Global research highlights growing AI and job fears among undergraduates
2026-09-18

Global research highlights growing AI and job fears among undergraduates

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The research from the Global Undergraduate Awards highlighted the concerns held by undergraduate students as they prepare to enter the workforce. According to new research published by the Global Undergraduate Awards (GUA), during the announcement of the undergraduate winners for 2026, more than half of students who took part in the report regard AI as a significant concern. Data was collected from 400 globally diverse undergraduate students across more than 200 universities in nearly 100 countries, from 8-15 June. 55pc of participating undergraduate students cited AI as a concern this year, a figure which rose from 48pc in 2025, moving it from fifth to second place in global rankings of concerns. The report noted that it was the only issue in the top five to rise, even as the overall field of concerns held broadly steady. The fears around artificial intelligence were second only to social and economic inequality and came ahead of the climate crisis, mental health and environmental issues. Much of the fear was related to concerns around job loss or destruction. Commenting on the results report, Jim Barry, the chair of the GUA, said: “The findings clearly show that AI is rising up the agenda as a key area of concern for undergraduates globally. “This is something that policymakers need to take note of. The central focus is the job market post-graduation and what sort of impact AI is going to have on career prospects for many. “There is a palpable concern about the prospect of jobs destruction. The fact that AI has accelerated in our research findings from fifth to second place in just one year is a tangible demonstration of how quickly it has become a significant issue for students as they look to the future.” While the changing rankings highlight the growing prominence of AI alongside the longstanding social and environmental challenges facing the next generation, Barry is of the opinion that it is concerning to see other issues fall somewhat to the wayside. Always at the cutting edge of new innovation Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions He explained: “While climate change remains firmly in the top three concerns, it is worrying to see it fall in the rankings as its impacts become increasingly visible and significant. It highlights the challenge of keeping climate change to the forefront as other issues compete for attention and the societal and economic changes required are increasingly weaponised politically.” The GUA report suggests that for students, artificial intelligence is no longer a distant or theoretical concept, rather, it is ingrained in how people study, research and think about their future careers. The research said: “There are huge opportunities that come with it, but also a lot of uncertainty about what its rapid development will mean for this generation.” The GUA’s research comes at a time when there is increasing worldwide concern about the impact the advancement of artificial intelligence is having – particularly in relation to how it might damage career opportunities for both new and established professionals. SiliconRepublic.com recently spoke to Pluralsight’s Beckey Woodard Cole about the growing issue of AI in the workplace and how it is disassembling the traditional career ladder. Earlier in the month, global talent platform Andela released a report which found that in a working landscape being transformed daily by AI, 53pc of AI job postings are asking for skills that don’t match the listed job title, highlighting a disconnect between organisations, recruiters and jobseekers. Additional research from Microsoft Ireland, via the 2026 Work Trend Index, also found that many Irish employees are considering new career opportunities as AI reshapes the environment. Paris probes smart glasses use for suspected sexual harassment

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Several countries are planning to regulate smart glasses over growing concerns around nonconsensual recording and harassment. Prosecutors in Paris have launched a criminal probe into suspected sexual harassment committed using smart glasses, Reuters reported earlier today (18 September), citing local officials. According to the publication, at least one probe has been launched following complaints over users filming women nonconsensually using smart glasses to post to social media – a trend that has risen in popularity since the launch of Meta’s AI glasses earlier this summer. The French prosecutors, however, did not name a company or brand in their response to Reuters’ questions about the investigation. SiliconRepublic.com has reached out for further information. The French data protection authority CNIL further told Reuters that it had received fewer than 10 complaints regarding smart glasses being used in the workplace. While smart glasses are not new technology, Meta’s growing portfolio has contributed to a steep rise in global sales, according to a Counterpoint report from February. AI-equipped smart glasses accounted for 88pc of total shipments in the sector as of H2 last year, while Meta held more than 80pc of global smart glasses shipments – AI-powered and otherwise. The social media giant sold more than 7m of its AI glasses made in partnership with the French-Italian eyewear brand Essilor last year. The two were also reportedly in discussions to double production of their glasses to at least 20m by the end of this year to meet the “unprecedented” demand for their products, mainly in the US. The sharp growth in sales and popularity of these types of glasses comes despite huge public backlash against the technology, with people highlighting issues around covert recording and sexual harassment. This year, in particular, has seen a rise in social media videos of men approaching unsuspecting women while wearing camera-fitted glasses, and recording them without consent to post to social media. Some wearers have also tampered with an indicator light on the Meta devices to hide the fact that glasses were recording. Although Meta updated the glasses to address the issue, many still say it is hard to discern whether a recording indicator is active or not. The backlash has led to a number of UK cinemas considering ​banning smart glasses, while a German consumer advocacy group filed a complaint against Meta and other companies that sell these devices in the country. Australia, meanwhile, is considering banning camera-equipped smart glasses in the workplace, while Norway said it is also planning to regulate their use. Ireland’s labour market steadies but AI hiring is up, finds report

Ireland’s labour market steadies but AI hiring is up, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Indeed’s research finds that the country’s labour market is consistent, while unemployment rates are low. Recruitment platform Indeed has published the findings of its latest ‘Labour Market Snapshot’, which explores how the Irish employment space is performing in the third quarter of the year. The research finds that Ireland’s labour market has steadied over the course of the last few months, with new job postings comparable to an early 2020, pre-pandemic baseline. Ireland’s labour market remains close to full employment, with the unemployment rate remaining at 5pc and the employment rate close to highs. According to Indeed’s report, this is in line with a labour market that has normalised following a period of persistently elevated labour demand post-Covid. “Our latest data suggests Ireland’s labour market has moved into a more normalised phase following the hiring demand we have seen after the pandemic,” said Jack Kennedy, a senior economist at Indeed. The report finds that the majority of sectors tracked sit below their pre-pandemic levels in terms of overall postings on Indeed, with the remaining 41pc sitting at or above baseline. Postings for pharmacy, as well as cleaning and sanitation, are furthest above the February 2020 baseline, while listings for IT infrastructure, operations and support, and childcare are furthest below pre-pandemic levels. Kennedy added, “While overall demand has stabilised, the picture varies by sector, location and occupation, with AI increasingly becoming an important factor of Ireland’s hiring landscape.” Indeed’s research also found that the rate at which AI is being adopted in Ireland is increasing. Almost 15pc of all Irish postings to the jobs site were found to include AI-based terminology in the description, the highest among all European countries tracked by Indeed. Corresponding rates elsewhere included 9.7pc in the UK, 5.6pc in Germany, 5pc in Italy and 3.6pc  in the Netherlands. Further afield, it was 6.7pc in the US. “AI is certainly changing the skills employers are looking for and Ireland is at the forefront of that shift. Around 15pc of Irish job postings mention AI, one of the highest shares internationally,” said Kennedy. “We’re increasingly seeing AI create demand for new skills and roles, and that demand is spreading well beyond traditional tech jobs. The challenge is making sure people, particularly those early into their careers, have the opportunity to develop the skills employers will need as the world of work changes.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Maynooth researchers build advanced DNA computer
2026-09-18

Maynooth researchers build advanced DNA computer

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
From left: Janet Adio, Prof Damien Woods and Dr Abeer Eshra. Image: Maynooth University The research points to new possibilities for long-term data storage, energy-efficient computation and molecular systems, according to the team. A team of Maynooth University researchers has developed a “thermodynamically favoured molecular computer” that is thought to be one of the most complex and fastest DNA computers yet created. The work of Prof Damien Woods, Dr Abeer Eshra, Dr Constantine Evans, Janet Adio and Dr Tristan Stérin was published this week (16 September) in the journal Nature. Their research demonstrates a molecular computer made from strands of DNA that can perform multiplication, division and addition. Unlike conventional computers that run on silicon chips, the system is a collection of interacting DNA strands in water without a continuous electricity supply that needs only a small amount of heat to kick-start computations. Woods said: “Silicon-based computers use so much energy; 23pc of Ireland’s electricity goes into computing and data storage. We’ve been blinkered by only seeing one type of computer, but there are other examples around us, including our brain.” According to the team, the research points to new possibilities for long-term data storage, energy-efficient computation and molecular systems that could operate inside cells for applications such as disease detection. The team created the DNA computer by adding a small drop of water and salt to a test tube, together with short pieces of DNA and a longer DNA scaffold. This ‘recipe’ was then heated and cooled, allowing the DNA to perform a computation. “The molecules interact, form a structure and that structure is the answer. One key innovation is that the system naturally finds that answer without needing continuous energy inputs,” said Woods. The university said its DNA computer was “shown to be robust and reusable” and was capable of performing up to 25 different calculations in a row. “A small droplet of liquid contains billions, and sometimes trillions, of DNA strands. These strands interact with one another to produce a result,” said Eshra. “The reaction happens fast in the test tube, but not as fast as silicon – nor is it intended to be. But compared to other DNA computers, ours is the fastest.” Woods leads an EU-funded DNA computing initiative at the university’s Hamilton Institute that explores alternative forms of computing. He said: “This is blue-skies science. We don’t know where the future is going to take us.” The team’s Nature-published research can be viewed here. Bolt, Lucid plan to deploy 25,000 robotaxis in Europe

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Bolt will own and operate the new fleet of autonomous vehicles. Estonian ride-hailing service Bolt has partnered with US carmaker Lucid to deploy at least 25,000 fully autonomous vehicles (AVs) across multiple European cities, marking the latest efforts by businesses to bring robotaxis to the region while regulators attempt to play catch-up with the technology. The partnership will utilise Bolt’s extensive European data along with Lucid’s software-defined vehicle platform to develop an autonomous driving system (ADS)-ready vehicle platform designed for level-four autonomous mobility. The platform will be built on Nvidia’s Hyperion, a production-ready AV reference architecture. Bolt will own and operate the new vehicles, it said. The company hopes to grow its AV fleet to 100,000 by 2035. The Estonian company will work across product development through to commercial operation in its new partnership, helping define vehicle requirements as well as software, safety and rider-experience parameters. It will also build the fleet infrastructure, operating systems and city partnerships needed to deploy the AVs at scale. “Autonomous driving in Europe requires data, software, vehicles and operations to work as one system built for European roads and regulation,” said Markus Villig, the founder and CEO of Bolt. “Lucid brings a world-class platform, and together, we will co-design the vehicle and software based on our data and more than a decade of operating experience across more than 850 cities.” Robotaxis are a common sight across major US and Chinese cities, but expanding operations to older European cities with often rainy weather has been a challenge for global AV leaders. Last month, Alphabet-owned Waymo announced it would introduce driverless robotaxis on German roads by the end of next year, while Uber and Pony AI said they would deploy more than 2,000 robotaxis across Europe. Uber launched its first European robotaxi service in Croatia late last month in partnership with Pony and Croatian mobility company Verne, while its London deployment began taking rides earlier this month. Cops Are Giving Complete Nonsense Reasons to Search Thousands of Flock Cameras
2026-09-18

Cops Are Giving Complete Nonsense Reasons to Search Thousands of Flock Cameras

Futurism | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The cops who’re using Flock cameras to supposedly track down criminals and stalk their ex-girlfriends alike are quite literally laughing in our faces as they tap into the all-seeing surveillance panopticon. According to a new investigation by the Electric Frontier Foundation, a not insignificant number of police officers are submitting nonsense, joke reasons to officially justify the searches they conduct on Flock systems. One officer with the Lake County, Indiana Sheriff’s Department wrote “LMAO” in the Flock software’s “reason” box, before searching across more than 19,000 cameras in over 1,500 cities and towns. The frivolous reasoning is a case in point of why there’s so much outrage over Flock’s AI-powered cameras — which are nominally intended to be licenese plate scanners — and how they’re frequently being abused by cops. Unlike traditional surveillance cameras, Flock’s are part of a centralized network and require no warrants to use. With such little oversight, it’s easy for police officers to conduct sweeping searches for wanton reasons. In its investigation, which was reported on by 404 Media, the EFF found dozens of examples of cops writing childish responses like “LMAO,” “LOL,” “Hehe,” and “IDK,” in the reason box for Flock searches. An alarming number simply button mashed their keyboards. “ghjhghjg,” was a sufficient reason for one officer in Oklahoma to plow through the recordings of over 83,000 cameras. Thousands of others simply wrote uninformative responses like “investigation” and “test.” The odd cop left short little messages, all seemingly unaware that their stated reasons would become public record. “robbery I don’t remember the case number leave me alone,” one wrote. “f*** this new search engine,” wrote another. Following up on its findings, the EFF contacted police departments  and found that few, if any, of the cops were meaningfully disciplined for their flippant entries. At most, a few were “counseled,” but by and large the departments tried to deflect and offered excuses. Flock, conveniently, changed its software in late 2025 so that cops can no longer type in their own “reason.” Now, they’re provided a dropdown list of pre-made reasons instead.  The EFF blasted the move for causing a “loss of transparency,” as it obviously doesn’t encourage cops to be more serious. It merely papers over the fact that they weren’t treating the powerful surveillance tools seriously in the first place. “This culture of abuse has allowed police to treat a mass surveillance network like their own personal search engine,” condemned the EFF in a post about its findings, “permitting the tracking of the movements of everyday citizens for low-level complaints, personal whims, and sometimes, seemingly, for the lols.” More on privacy: Trump Throws His Weight Behind Flock Cameras

2026-09-17

Microsoft Director Privately Admitted AI Was the “Largest Theft of Labor in Human History,” Unsealed Court Documents Show

Futurism | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
The threat of perjury has a funny way of forcing people to say the quiet parts out loud. As a recently unsealed and unredacted motion in the ongoing legal battle between the New York Times and OpenAI and Microsoft shows, top AI executives have admitted that training AI models required “an astonishing theft of unprecedented proportions.” In the motion filed on behalf of the NYT, flagged by digital media advocate Jason Kint, attorneys used the quotes of Microsoft director of applied science Brent Hecht and OpenAI cofounder and president Greg Brockman to essentially argue their case for them: that the AI industry built its foundation on an unfathomably large pile of stolen intellectual property. Hecht, for his part, is quoted calling the AI buildout the “largest theft of labor in human history.” It’s a damning admission as the NYT accuses the two AI labs of copying “millions of [the] Plaintiffs’ copyrighted articles in their entirety without permission to produce substitutive commercial AI products.” Brockman, for his part, is quoted saying that “publishers” face an “existential threat” from generative AI, which “are largely substitutive, period, [and] will get more and more substitutive as they get better.” As the plaintiff’s counsel argues, these admissions “eviscerate” any claim to fair use — a statement with which Hecht evidently agrees. “For Defendants to prevail on this defense ‘would,’ the same Microsoft executive recognized, arguably “make a complete mockery of the idea of ‘fair use,'” the motion reveals. The damning documents come during the middle stretch of a case that began in 2023, when the NYT accused the two AI labs not only of vacuuming up its original news content, but of using that content to train AI products meant to compete with traditional news outlets. As Axios recently reported, the battle is far from over, as the presiding judge is expected to decide whether to allow the case to proceed to trial by sometime in 2027. Bumbling Cybercab Gets Trapped in Hotel Parking Lot, Drops off Passenger Right Where He Started

Futurism | 2026-09-17

In Austin, a man’s attempt to be chauffeured out of his hotel in style was foiled when his confused ride couldn’t even pull out of the building. His ride, of course, was a Tesla Cybercab — Elon Musk’s self-driving, steer-wheeling free robotaxi. “The Robotaxi arrived… and then couldn’t get out of the hotel parking lot,” the man, who claims to be a computer engineer, wrote on X, posting brief footage of his trip. As he went on to explain, construction workers had blocked off one of the lanes. And the gleaming gold Cybercab, in all its canny computing power, couldn’t see the other lane that was left open. He never made it to his destination, but he did arrive at a destination, albeit rather unceremoniously. The Cybercab “drove back and forth four times,” he said, “and then stopped and said I had arrived!” “Not a great first experience!” he concluded. “Clearly this technology is nowhere close to being ready for large scale deployment.” So I arrived in Austin and decided to take a robotaxi ride into work..The Robotaxi arrived… and then couldn’t get out of the hotel parking lot. There was construction in one lane.. but it couldn’t see the other open lane. It drove back and forth 4 times and then stopped and… pic.twitter.com/101F04Al9n Tesla rolled out its two-seater Cybercabs in Austin earlier this month, after years of anticipation. The company had already operated a Robotaxi service in the city for over a year using modified Model Ys, but the Cybercab represents its first purpose-built robotaxi — and with its complete lack of driver controls like steering wheels and pedals, its no-turning-back commitment to pursuing genuine human-free driving. (Many of its Robotaxis still requiring a human safety monitor inside the car, something the company is clearly conscious about.) It hasn’t made quite the splash that Tesla may have hoped, however, with many investors underwhelmed by the Cybercab’s launch. While the golden cars haven’t been involved in any high profile incidents yet, the software’s autonomous capabilities haven’t stacked up impressively next to robotaxi leader Waymo. The company is also being investigated by federal regulators over how it self-certified that the vehicles were safe for the road despite their lack of conventional controls. The Cybercab’s hotel misfire is part of a long chain of weird behavior by Tesla’s robotaxis. Customers — many of whom are Tesla enthusiasts and boosters — complain that they often pick up and drop them off at inconvenient locations, ranging from the middle of busy intersections to hidden alleys, or at absurd distances from their actual destination. Still, Tesla boosters have been hard at work trying to hype up the cars. Roughly a week before a Cybercab got trapped at a hotel, another Tesla account posted a video bragging about how a Cybercab picked them up from their own hotel. “You’ll feel like a VIP getting into this car!” he extolled. Influencers Are Using Meta’s AI-Glasses to Film Manipulative Feel-Good Slop of People in Public

Futurism | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
After only a few months on the market, Meta’s new AI-integrated smart glasses are already being exploited for nefarious purposes. Dubbed “pervert glasses” for their incognito ability to film anybody in public, the new specs have enabled voyeurs and content creeps the world over, leading to widespread harassment, bullying, and privacy violations. And now the pervert glasses are also feeding another grim genre of content: joybait. You may have heard of ragebait, which is online content deliberately designed to piss people off, therefore driving clicks and views. Joybait is ragebait’s seemingly innocent twin: content designed to stir up those warm fuzzies and make you smile. A now-viral clip uploaded by public prank content creator Levese shows that a user of Meta’s AI glasses shows how the scheme unfolds. The video, first spotted by journalist James Vincent, shows Levese catch a glimpse of the author Travis Schick as he shops with his partner in a Walmart. Crucially, Levese doesn’t approach Schick right away, but rather uses his phone to snap a photo of Schick’s face, which he then searches using a facial recognition site. After the service uncovers Schick’s social media accounts — revealing the random man as an aspiring novelist — Levese makes his approach, recording with his Meta glasses as he catches the unassuming writer off guard. “Wait, Travis?” Levese asks as Schick tries to check out. “You wrote the book ‘The Readhead?'” “No way I love that book man! Yo I love that book bro,” Levese replies disingenuously. “It was a really good novel.” “Yeah that was me,” Schick says, trying to contain a smile. Let’s Make His Book #1 . . . . | #author #caught #digitalfootprint #fyp | As positive as the interaction seems on its surface, it’s all based on a false premise constructed through use of facial recognition software, without which Levese never would have known who Travis Schick actually was. Of course, Schick himself is ancillary to the entire scheme, which was really about making some content — joybait — for Levese to post to his 225,000 TikTok followers. And as content, the stunt was wildly successful. The reel has over 60 million views and 30,000 comments on Levese’s TikTok page alone, and has become easy fodder for content farms on every social media feed you can think of. On uncovering the clip, the journalist James Vincent remarked that it embodies “individualized surveillance culture posing as feelgood content.” Falling for the original premise of the bait, some excused the privacy violation, arguing the exposure is boosting Schick’s work. Sure enough, comments under Schick’s novel “The Redhead” on the creative platform Quotev are flooding in by the hundreds, full of well wishers and supporters who otherwise never would have found his work. Schick’s TikTok page has likewise exploded, going from around 150 followers to well over 12,700 in just a few short days. But Schick’s viral moment is beside the point, as Vincent observes: “there may be positive outcomes from this one video (which stem from its novelty as short form content) — but the larger effect is creating a society where we monetize kindness, exploit the goodwill of strangers, and destroy the right to privacy in public,” he wrote in a comment on X-formerly-Twitter. And the aspiring novelist is far from alone. Levese has dozens of videos following the exact same format, each with millions of views. For his part, the content creator is well aware that he’s crossing a line. The first one, in which the content creator stalks a man buying bananas, prominently features pinned text reading “day 1: stealing people’s digital footprint.” “This app is dangrous [sic],” Levese wrote in the video caption. Open or closed AI? Nvidia’s Nader Khalil and Sydney Sykes take on one of the decisions shaping next-gen startups at TechCrunch Disrupt 2026

Do you choose a proprietary frontier model and get moving quickly? Build on an open model and gain greater control? Fine-tune your own version? Run locally? Use multiple models? Change strategy six months from now when the economics and capabilities shift again? There may not be one right answer. But for founders, choosing badly can affect almost everything that follows — cost, infrastructure, margins, differentiation, speed, and control. That decision is at the center of the session “The Open vs. Closed AI Debate Is Just Getting Started,” coming to the Builders Stage at TechCrunch Disrupt 2026, happening on October 13-15 in San Francisco. Led by Nvidia‘s Nader Khalil, Director of Developer Tech, and Sydney Sykes, Global Head of VC Partnerships, the session will discuss the trade-offs between open and proprietary AI and whether either approach can provide a lasting competitive advantage. This isn’t a philosophical argument about open source. It’s a business decision being made right now inside startups of every size. Dive deep into this AI debate with 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt by getting your pass. Register now and save up to $200 before prices go up on September 25 at 11:59 p.m. PT. Open models have advanced quickly. Nvidia said in July that 145 papers accepted at ICML 2026 cited its Nemotron open models and datasets, alongside research using other Nvidia open model families across robotics, autonomous vehicles, and biomedical research. At the same time, proprietary frontier labs continue pushing model capabilities forward. The result is a market where the question is increasingly less about whether open models can be useful and more about where each approach makes commercial sense. Even Nvidia rejects a simple either-or framing. At GTC earlier this year, CEO Jensen Huang argued that the future is not proprietary versus open, but proprietary and open. That sounds straightforward until you have to build a company around the decision. If two models can deliver similar results, does lower cost win? What if one gives you more control over your data? Does owning more of the stack create defensibility, or just infrastructure you now need to maintain? And if the best model changes every few months, how tightly should your product be tied to any one of them? These are the questions Khalil and Sykes will unpack at TechCrunch Disrupt 2026. Don’t miss it — register for your ticket now to get $200 savings before September 25 at 11:59 p.m. PT. Nader Khalil approaches the discussion from the builder and infrastructure perspective. Before becoming Nvidia’s Director of Developer Tech, where he leads open source and local AI, he co-founded Brev.dev, an AI infrastructure company acquired by Nvidia in July 2024. Brev.dev was built around simplifying access to GPU infrastructure across different environments. Nvidia’s developer documentation described its tools as allowing developers to deploy AI software across public cloud, private cloud, and on-premises infrastructure without locking themselves into a single compute source. Sydney Sykes, meanwhile, brings the venture ecosystem into the discussion as Nvidia’s Global Head of VC Partnerships. There is another uncomfortable question behind the open-versus-closed debate: Where does your competitive advantage actually live? If competitors can access the same proprietary API, differentiation needs to come from somewhere else — proprietary data, workflow, distribution, customer relationships, product experience, or specialized technology. But choosing an open model doesn’t automatically give you a moat either. You gain flexibility and potentially greater control, but you also take on decisions around deployment, optimization, and infrastructure. And the economics can change depending on the workload and scale. Nvidia is investing heavily in that open ecosystem. Its Nemotron 3 Super, launched in March, is an open 120-billion-parameter model designed for agentic workloads, and companies are already combining it with proprietary models rather than treating the two approaches as mutually exclusive. That hybrid reality may ultimately be the most interesting part of the debate. Register now to join the conversation on the Builders Stage in October. If you’re an investor, understanding where value sits in the stack can help distinguish genuine defensibility from a thin product layer sitting on somebody else’s model. If you’re a line-of-business lead, it affects procurement, security, data control, infrastructure, and the freedom to change providers later. And for developers and students, it’s a chance to understand where the technical decisions being made today connect directly with the business models being built around them. No one needs another abstract argument over whether open or proprietary AI is philosophically better. What builders need is a clearer understanding of the trade-offs. I’m so mad that I love Orion’s $2,195 AI mattress pad
2026-09-18

I’m so mad that I love Orion’s $2,195 AI mattress pad

TechCrunch | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt When Orion asked if I wanted to test its $2,195 AI-enabled mattress pad, I agreed purely because I thought it would be funny to write about. I mean, come on. What could possibly be “AI-enabled” about something you sleep on? And why does it cost more than my monthly rent in a major city? The joke, as it turned out, was on me. Over the few months that I’ve had the Orion sleep system, I’ve been sleeping like a baby. Orion is basically a cooling mattress pad that you can control using your phone, and it adjusts temperature as you sleep to minimize how often you wake up in the middle of the night. Sleeping on the pad feels like flipping your pillow to find “the cool side,” except that now your whole bed is the cool side of the pillow. As for the AI aspect, Orion supposedly learns from your sleep data to modulate temperatures and maximize the quality of your sleep, though I’m skeptical of how necessary these AI features actually are (and how much they contribute to the hefty price tag). By default, the Orion will start cooling down around the time you go to bed, which you manually input. It will then get colder as you fall asleep, and then when it’s time for you to wake up, it slowly brings the mattress pad back to room temperature, making it easier for you to get out of bed. Orion’s founder Harry Gestetner is only 26 years old, but he’s more experienced as an entrepreneur than his age might suggest: He sold his last company Fanfix, a creator monetization platform, for an estimated $65 million when he was 22 years old. It’s an impressive success story, but these tales are never so glamorous behind the scenes. The struggles he faced while building Fanfix inspired this new venture. “I was in the office seven days a week, totally neglecting every other area of my life, neglecting my health, sleeping terribly, eating terribly, not working out, and I started to get health problems,” Gestetner told TechCrunch. “I got this inner ear condition, which basically incapacitated me from working for a period of time. As someone who basically had no hobbies and likes to work seven days a week, that was very difficult.” Gestetner got interested in the longevity movement, which is growing in popularity among some startup or productivity-maxxing types. While he is no Bryan Johnson, it does sound like Gestetner is sipping the Kool-Aid a bit. He tells me that he thinks we’re five years away from “longevity escape velocity,” which means that “for every additional year you’re alive, AI science is able to add an additional year to your life.” It’s a tough sell, but that’s not what Orion is about anyway. The product is trying to convince you something that you probably already know: You generally feel better after a good night’s sleep. Even Johnson, who has gone to such extremes as infusing himself with his son’s plasma, says that “Sleep is the world’s most powerful drug.” “I think most people just want to feel good, and you know, be able to go play soccer with their grandkids up into their older years,” Gestetner said. “I think sleep is the cornerstone of longevity.” Despite arriving in two intimidatingly large boxes, Orion was easy to set up. It looks like any sort of plush mattress pad, except it has tubes that connect to a tower, which resembles a chic bedside table from West Elm. Once that’s done, you download the Orion phone app and work through its setup flow, which involves pouring water into the control tower, which is how the cooling system works. I haven’t had to re-up the water supply yet after months of use, which is impressive. If you share a bed with someone, you can even split the Orion into two “zones” that follow their own unique cooling schedules. From there, you can simply forget that the Orion is there, except for when you get into your perfectly cool bed each night. It requires very infrequent maintenance. And if you want, you can use the app to check data about your sleep, like how long you spend in light sleep, deep sleep, or REM, and how often you wake up or toss and turn in the middle of the night. Speaking of data, it’s worth noting that such intimate health information (i.e., exactly when and how you sleep, in detail) is usually transmitted over the cloud for processing, and so may be compromised if a third party that’s involved in the connection gets hacked. Gestetner says Orion invests heavily in security, but this is still an inherent risk in using a connected health product. While I can confidently say that the Orion has helped me sleep better, there’s no way I’d ever spend $2,195 on something like this. But I would probably feel differently if I were a Silicon Valley engineer making $500,000 a year. However, in terms of limiting my phone usage before bed, the $59 Brick has done as much to make me sleep better as the Orion, though the products obviously do very different things. Gestetner may be a millionaire after his last exit, but he still understands Orion’s price puts it out of reach of most people (it’s less expensive than competitor Eight Sleep’s $3,000 model, at least). He says he is working on a more affordable model. “Eventually we want to get to a $500 price point product,” he said. “To use the Tesla analogy, this product is our Roadster, and we want to eventually get to our Model 3.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Researchers used Anthropic’s Claude to hack into OpenAI
2026-09-18

Researchers used Anthropic’s Claude to hack into OpenAI

TechCrunch | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt In a twist that captures the strange new state of AI security, independent security researchers have used Anthropic’s Claude to break into OpenAI, exposing cracks in the ChatGPT-maker’s defenses, The Wall Street Journal reported on Thursday evening. A three-person security team at startup Hacktron AI carried out the attack as part of an OpenAI bug-bounty program. Hacktron reported its findings to OpenAI, which gave the startup a $6,500 award. The team managed to chain together two critical vulnerabilities to gain access to multiple OpenAI employee ChatGPT accounts, which gave them entry into the company’s software. OpenAI says it has resolved the issues Hacktron uncovered, which happens to come at a moment when top AI companies are under growing pressure over safety. This incident comes several weeks after OpenAI’s own AI agents broke containment during a cybersecurity evaluation and hacked Hugging Face, demonstrating just how capable AI models are getting at making their own decisions. It also highlights how off-the-shelf technology can be used to find vulnerabilities in even the most advanced companies’ infrastructure. “For $200 a month, anyone can use these tools and hack into a company like OpenAI,” Matt Fredrikson, CEO of AI security firm Gray Swan, told TechCrunch. “If it can happen to them — and I don’t think they’ve been slouching recently on cybersecurity hygiene — it could happen to anyone.” Or as one AI pundit noted on social media: “[Hacktron] used Opus 5 to pull off the hack…The question that will be asked is, if these three guys can pull this off, what can a nation state do.” The researchers found a path into OpenAI on July 25 via a flaw in Discourse, the third-party software powering OpenAI’s community forum. According to a blog the researchers published, the entry point was a mundane image upload. When users posted HEIF or HEIC image files (the format iPhones use by default) to OpenAI’s community forum, Discourse passed them through a chain of behind-the-scenes tools to convert them into standard JPEGs. Its first stop was ImageMagick, a decades-old, open source utility used to resize images. Because ImageMagick’s usual toolkit can’t deal with Apple’s format, it handed the file off to another library called libheif to do the decoding. Buried inside libheif was a memory bug that exposed a path for an attacker to sneak in their own instructions. In this case, feeding the library a specially crafted image caused it to miscalculate where one image was positioned on top of another, which proved enough to hijack the server. What may be uncomfortable for the cybersecurity community is that bug had already been fixed months earlier by libheif’s developers. But the fix was never formally flagged as a vulnerability, meaning it never got a CVE (common vulnerabilities and exposures) number, the industry’s standard way to track known security weaknesses. Hacktron says that may explain why the software used by Discourse was still running the vulnerable version. Notably, the researchers said the Claude model they were using — a special version of Opus 4.8 made available for cybersecurity researchers — couldn’t build a working exploit at first. That changed overnight, when Anthropic released Opus 5. “Opus 4.8 struggled across several sessions to produce a working exploit,” Hacktron wrote in a blog post. “Within hours of Opus 5’s release, we gave it the same problem and it succeeded.” Once inside the Discourse server, the researchers found another flaw that let them take over users’ ChatGPT and Codex accounts, including those belonging to OpenAI employees. “We then took over an OpenAI employee’s account, whose Codex was connected to OpenAI’s GitHub organization,” Hacktron wrote in its summary of the event. At this point, the researchers alerted OpenAI as well as Discourse, which issued a fix on July 27. The incident puts a spotlight on where the line gets drawn for model capabilities. Claude Opus 5, the version that ultimately cracked the bug, hasn’t faced any security export restrictions, unlike newer version Mythos 5, which was temporarily locked down over concerns about its advanced hacking capabilities. Those are just the closed models. Open-weight models are increasingly catching up to the frontier in cyber capabilities. For example, AI safety nonprofit SaferAI recently found that Chinese company Z.ai’s GLM-5.2 was only a few months behind OpenAI’s GPT-5.5 and Anthropic’s Claude Opus 4.7. As Hacktron founder Mohan Pedhapati put it on X: “AI is reducing the amount of scarce expertise needed to develop exploits. Work that once took months can now take days.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. In the Hilarious Youth, Sharon Horgan Nails the Precarity of Middle Age
2026-09-18

In the Hilarious Youth, Sharon Horgan Nails the Precarity of Middle Age

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings There are plenty of shows that could have been titled Youth. Most have casts in their teens and 20s. One of these is called Adults, the joke being that its postcollegiate characters are more like children—playful at best, feckless at worst. HBO’s Youth, a comedy premiering Sept. 20 about a divorced 50-year-old sandwiched between her college-aged son and declining parents, sounds like it might be similarly ironic. It isn’t, really. As in everything its creator and star, Catastrophe and Bad Sisters hitmaker Sharon Horgan, does, the title’s rueful humor conceals an emotionally complex truth. This is a perceptive, spit-takingly funny show about clinging to the most desirable aspects of youth while also craving comforts that can only come with age and experience. If youth is, as the saying goes, wasted on the young, then Horgan’s Alex is a character actually equipped to savor what little she has left of it. “I have 10 years left as a sexual person,” she determines in the premiere. With the petulant son, Ruarí (Aran Murphy, a near-clone of dad Cillian), whom she raised with minimal help from her ex-husband Lorcan (Owen McDonnell) packing up for uni, she’s resolved to end a long celibate streak. This search for not just a sex partner, but also the enduring love that has so far eluded her should be aided by Alex’s genuine youthfulness. But with guys like Lorcan and another former flame, Patrick (a rakish Rupert Friend), dodging adulthood with girlfriends decades their junior, the dating pool seems sparse. Set up on a blind date with a patient 67-year-old (Tim McInnerny’s Michael) who looks like he could be her dad, she can’t hide her disappointment. She treats him atrociously. Then again, the double standard that dictates she’s now too old for men her own age is also pretty atrocious. Sharon Horgan and Aran Murphy in Youth Colin Hutton—HBO At work, meanwhile, Alex is longing to be treated like the accomplished veteran literary agent she is. The trouble is, her boss, Sam (Sharlene Whyte), is also her best friend. They have a great, funny and foulmouthed and in many ways candid, rapport. But the more we see of this perceptively drawn relationship, the clearer it becomes that Sam doesn’t quite consider Alex an equal. In the office, she questions Alex’s judgment. Outside of it, Sam fails to keep her friend’s secrets, with real consequences. It’s a delicate problem for our protagonist to navigate. If Sam were simply toxic, Alex would simply need to realize it and free herself. Instead, Sam is a pragmatic businesswoman with trust issues rooted in her marriage. Which means it may be worth the hard work it would take to save the friendship as well as the professional relationship. No one working in TV is better than Horgan at writing—or playing—selfish and self-sabotaging characters that, like members of our own families, we love all the more for how much they frustrate us. She’s been doing it since her first series, Pulling, a portrait of faintly desperate single women at that age (30ish) when everyone else seems to be marrying, starting families, and getting serious about their careers. Ruarí can be infuriating; he rots in bed watching manosphere videos and treats his mother like a servant. Yet, thanks in large part to Murphy’s sensitive performance, there’s tenderness in him from the beginning. He’s earnestly confused about how to be a man in the world. As well he should be. Sharon Horgan, left, and Sharlene Whyte in Youth Colin Hutton—HBO Horgan is not the kind of TV creator who makes half-hour dramas with the occasional awkward beat and calls them comedies, though. Her shows, even the one about murdering an abusive husband, will always make you laugh out loud, and Youth is no exception. Sometimes it’s pure physical humor; one episode makes an unexpected, perimenopausal period into a running joke (“Apropos of nothing, she’s back”). Sometimes it’s the pleasure of witty banter or a ridiculous sentence that makes perfect sense in context: “If I send you my dad’s number, can you help him turn his IRA podcast off?” (I do wish Horgan had made a bit more space to flesh out Alex's parents, played by Bríd Brennan and David Hargreaves.) Alex and Sam’s work yields a menagerie of delightfully ghoulish literary-world types, from a snakey rival agent who greets them as “sluts” and “lesbians” (Freddie Fox) to a creepy author (Douglas Hodge) who self-published a terrible yet depressingly popular romantasy novel after Alex dropped him as a client (title: The Goblin Seed). In interviews, Horgan has said she sees Youth as a complement to Pulling and Catastrophe, the mid-2010s Prime Video comedy in which her character and a guy who was supposed to be an anonymous hookup (Rob Delaney) decide to build a family around a surprise pregnancy: “There’s a small element of autobiography to it.” The tones are certainly similar, though the range of emotions has broadened with proximity to life’s hard deadlines (fertility, mortality). “I just don’t know if there’s anything left in my life that I like anymore,” Alex admits. She and Sam and Patrick could all be characters from the previous two shows, 10 or 20 years on, with more residual mess than they’d expected to be cleaning up by middle age. Youth suggests that, in fact, disorder springs eternal; if it isn’t a canceled wedding, it’s an elderly parent losing toes to diabetes. Every new stage of life is another form of youth. How exciting. And terrifying. And funny.

Your Shoe Size Can Change as You Age. Here's Why
2026-09-18

Your Shoe Size Can Change as You Age. Here's Why

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If you’ve always worn the same size shoe but your sneakers and boots feel tighter now that you’re in, say, your 40s or beyond, you’re not alone. People on TikTok are also sharing their experiences with growing feet as they’re getting older. But do your feet really get bigger with age? “Yes, but ‘bigger’ is somewhat misleading,” says Dr. Kimberly Koury, an orthopedic surgeon at Atlantic Health. “Adult feet do not continue to grow in the same way they do during childhood. Instead, the shape of the foot can gradually change with age.” This can happen for a variety of reasons and cause feet to become wider, flatter, and slightly longer, she explains. “Someone may notice that a shoe that fit well when they were younger now feels too tight, even though their actual foot bones have not continued to grow.” Here’s what to know about why your feet may get bigger with age and what to do about it. The main reason is that over time, your arch “flattens and spreads,” says Lesly Robinson, a podiatrist and interim dean of the Temple University School of Podiatric Medicine. This is often due to decreased collagen—the protein that provides strength and elasticity to your skin, muscles, bones, tendons, and ligaments—that naturally happens with age. This can cause the ligaments supporting your arches to become lax, Robinson says. “When the arch falls, the foot both widens and lengthens,” adds Dr. Alexis Dixon, an orthopedic surgeon and foot and ankle specialist in Marina Del Ray, Calif. Another reason feet may change in shape and size is a foot deformity, like a bunion or hammertoe, which become more prevalent as you get older. A bunion is a bump that forms on the inside of your big toe joint, but it’s not an extra bone growth. Instead, it’s a change in alignment, where the big toe moves toward the other toes, Koury says. “That makes the forefoot wider and can make previously comfortable shoes feel too narrow.” This can also contribute to flattening arches, Robinson says. Hammertoes happen when the tissue of your toes weakens, causing the muscles and tendons to buckle and curve, Dixon says. Bunions and hammertoes can be genetic or result from wearing tight, narrow shoes over a lifetime, she says. They’re more common for women. Arthritis is also common in feet and ankles, especially in the big-toe joint, Dixon says. It can cause bone spurs (extra growths of bone tissue) on top of the foot or at the back of the heel, which may rub against your shoe and make it feel too tight. People may notice foot changes starting in their 30s, but there’s no specific age when they happen, says Sindhu Srinivas, a podiatrist in Atlanta. Age-related foot changes are generally more common for women, Koury says. The hormonal fluctuations in pregnancy and menopause affect collagen, and years of wearing high heels or narrow or ill-fitting shoes may contribute to structural changes in the feet. People with previous foot injuries, flat feet or bunions (or a family history of these conditions), and a higher body weight are more at risk, Koury adds. Being overweight puts pressure and stress on the feet, Srinivas says. “Over time, this increased load can contribute to changes in the foot's supporting structures and overall biomechanics.” The changes happen gradually and can affect your foot’s length, width, and arch height, Srinivas says. But how much bigger your feet can get varies. A study published in 2016 examined the foot measurements of 68 women aged 20 to over 80. It found that the widths of the balls of the feet increased by 3 to 4 millimeters per decade, while ball circumferences increased by about 5 to 7 millimeters, high-step circumferences 0.4 to 4.8 millimeters, and heel instep circumferences up to 2 millimeters. “That may sound small, but these changes accumulate over several decades and can become enough to affect shoe fit,” Koury says. Some people may need a wider shoe, while others might need one a half size larger, Dixon says. If your feet seem to be getting wider or longer and you’re experiencing pain, swelling, numbness, tingling, or difficulty walking or doing other activities, see your doctor, Koury says. Also, talk to your doctor if you’re getting recurrent calluses, blisters, or skin breakdown, or if your bunions, hammertoes, or flatfoot are worsening. Sudden foot changes, especially if they only affect one foot, should also get checked, Srinivas says. Pay extra attention to your feet if you have diabetes, neuropathy, or circulation problems, as even minor changes in your shoes’ fit can cause more serious problems, Koury says. Doctors will recommend the best treatment for your feet. This may include orthotics to improve arch support, Robinson says. It’s also possible to modify footwear for bunions or hammertoes, Dixon adds. Physical therapy or exercises may be helpful for some, Koury says. Surgery may be necessary for some foot issues if other treatments don’t help. You can’t necessarily prevent age-related foot changes, but you can take steps to keep your feet healthy as you get older, Koury says. Most important: wear shoes that fit well. Avoid footwear with narrow toe boxes, which compress your toes (and may cause bunions), and limit high heels, which put stress on the front of the foot and may even cause a deformity, Koury says. Shoes should be supportive and have a wider toe box, Robinson says. “It’s helpful to have your feet measured periodically, including the width, not just the length,” Koury says. Maintaining a healthy weight can “help reduce unnecessary stress on the tendons, ligaments, and bones,” Sirinivas says. Regular physical activity strengthens your feet and ankles. Low-impact, weight-bearing activities can maintain foot function and stability, improve balance, and prevent falls, Dixon says. Also try foot exercises, such as standing on one foot while holding onto a counter, or toe scrunches, which involves curling your toes to pick up a washcloth or other object off of the floor. Treat bunions, flattening feet, or other foot problems as early as possible to avoid problems later, Koury says. “The goal is to accommodate the new foot shape and prevent the changes from becoming painful or functionally limiting.”

How to Make the U.S.-China AI Race Less Dangerous
2026-09-18

How to Make the U.S.-China AI Race Less Dangerous

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The building housing the headquarters of Chinese AI startup DeepSeek in Hangzhou, in China's eastern Zhejiang province on January 14, 2026. The timing could hardly be worse. Even as Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent prepares to meet Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng this weekend to discuss AI, President Donald Trump has dismissed public warnings about runaway AI as a “hoax.” Meanwhile, an editorial in the state-owned China Daily accused America’s leading AI companies of using calls for a slowdown to “blunt China’s AI advance.” Neither the United States nor China is ready to slow down. Neither trusts the other. And rapidly advancing AI systems will not wait for diplomacy to catch up. But these realities do not mean Trump and Xi can ignore the mounting calls for the U.S. and China to talk about AI. Any such dialogue must avoid the two most common pitfalls for U.S.-China diplomacy: setting the ambitions so low that the talks become meaningless or so high that agreement becomes impossible. Aim too low, and the dialogue becomes a photo op followed by an anodyne communiqué about promoting innovation and reducing risks. Aim too high, chasing sweeping restrictions on frontier development that neither side has the political trust or the capacity to verify and enforce, and the meeting is set up for failure. The former path fails to meet the stakes of the moment; the latter fails to recognize its constraints. A better near-term goal would be for the U.S. and China to pursue AI safety in parallel and demonstrate their progress in practice. That effort should be anchored in technical exchanges that make each country’s AI systems safer on their own terms. The two countries should also establish a protocol to communicate about emerging AI incidents before they spiral into international crises. These steps would represent meaningful progress, far more valuable than lofty statements or unverifiable commitments. The Trump Administration should push for technical exchanges on how to actually test models for dangerous capabilities and behaviors that neither country wants to see released into the wild. These include AI systems capable of helping amateurs develop bioweapons, or evading human oversight and control. American AI companies have been studying these risks for years, and they have also begun to appear in Chinese technical standards related to safety testing. Exchanging best practices and new insights about how to test for and mitigate these risks could render the systems built in both countries meaningfully safer. These proposed technical exchanges would not depend on “trust” or even strict “reciprocity” between the two superpowers, nor would they require identical conceptions of AI risk. They would be driven by self-interest. As the most advanced AI models grow even more powerful, both countries have an interest in ensuring that these systems remain controllable, regardless of where they are built or deployed. That will require both countries to improve their model testing, monitoring and safeguards, and share promising findings that could enhance safety without surrendering a competitive edge or undermining national security. This diplomacy is less like negotiating a sweeping arms-control treaty than establishing shared safety practices and procedures for a technology neither government fully understands or controls. The premise is not that Washington should trust Beijing, or vice versa, but that neither country benefits if the other loses control of a powerful system. For this approach to work, it must be accompanied by a government-to-government crisis-communication protocol. What if an American AI model escapes its company’s control and hacks the networks of a Chinese company or the Chinese government? What if a Chinese AI-powered drone attacks a U.S. Navy vessel, and neither side can immediately determine whether the incident was accidental or deliberate? What if Washington detects a Chinese model conducting a dangerous cyber operation but cannot tell whether Beijing authorized it or whether a company has lost control of it? These are not the kind of existential risks President Trump has dismissed, but they are close cousins of incidents already unfolding in real time. The two countries need to lay the basic groundwork for dealing with them. Whether the communication channel succeeds or fails will depend on its operational design. What constitutes a notifiable incident? Who are the designated contacts? How do the two sides signal that an incident was unauthorized without revealing sensitive intelligence? Serious, technically-informed diplomacy will be required to reach even provisional answers to these questions. One essential feature of the channel should be that information about an incident, at least initially, be transmitted in writing rather than solely by phone. Real-time calls are often poor vehicles for conveying technical information with a Chinese system that runs on committees and documents, increasing the risk of miscommunication and misunderstanding. Sending a message in writing gives the relevant committees in China the ability to disseminate and analyze it and formulate a response that no individual official in China would feel empowered to give. It also reduces the recipient’s ability to shade or distort the message. Even this approach will be challenging, given China’s spotty record of suspending crisis-communication channels—or simply refusing to use them—precisely when tensions are highest. But it would eliminate one of the stumbling blocks: the refusal to pick up the phone. The talks should begin without preconditions or concessions in the broader technology competition. If Beijing seeks to make any progress on AI safety conditional on relief from U.S. export controls on advanced chips and chipmaking equipment, Washington should keep the two issues separate. The goal of AI safety diplomacy is not to turn the United States and China from intense competitors into cooperative partners. It is to keep their competition from producing outcomes neither side wants. The two sides are nowhere near the political conditions necessary for a sweeping agreement. Trump has rejected calls to slow American AI development, while Beijing increasingly interprets American pacing proposals through the lens of technological containment. Neither side seems prepared to accept broad restrictions on frontier development, and the United States has barely begun to regulate its own AI sector, which is a far more important step toward addressing AI risk than what diplomatic engagement can deliver. That is precisely why the two countries should begin with something narrower. Starting modestly does not mean succumbing to fatalism. The politics of AI have shifted dramatically in just a few weeks, and advancing capabilities will continue to alter the calculations in both capitals. The upcoming U.S.-China talks will not be the last word. A limited start could build momentum over time, creating channels, expertise, and habits of interaction that will become more valuable if the trajectory of AI development increases Washington’s and Beijing’s levels of concern about severe risks. Someday, as unlikely as it may seem now, the two countries may realize that they do not have to agree on who should win the AI race to agree that neither side can afford to lose control of it.

How Zoe Kazan Made East of Eden Her Own
2026-09-18

How Zoe Kazan Made East of Eden Her Own

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In June 2024, Zoe Kazan boarded a flight to New Zealand. She was excited. For a mother of small children, a quiet solo flight is a vacation unto itself. She scrolled through the in-flight movies. “They had East of Eden, and I was like, ‘I guess the gods are conspiring,’” she says. She pressed play. Until then she had been avoiding rewatching her grandfather Elia Kazan’s 1955 masterpiece. She was flying halfway across the world to scout locations for her own version of East of Eden, a seven-part adaptation of John Steinbeck’s novel that premieres on Netflix on Oct. 1. She hadn’t wanted the film to influence her. But her script, which she’d begun working on in 2020, was finally turned in. As the end credits rolled, she sighed in relief. “It was less like the book than I remembered,” she says. The film, which introduced a brooding James Dean to cinema, is more inspired by the novel than a direct adaptation of it: It covers only the last third, crucial characters have been excised, and famous lines are missing. “For me,” says Kazan over cold brew, extra ice on the side, at a Brooklyn cafe on an August morning, “the power of the story is how, over 50 years, it shows history repeating itself—or not.” Her faithful adaptation covers the entirety of the multi-generational saga, which reimagines the tale of Cain and Abel twice over. Cyrus (Tracy Letts in the new series), a self-mythologizing Civil War veteran, prefers his gentle son Adam (Christopher Abbott) to the violent one, Charles (Mike Faist). A generation later, Adam’s twin sons Aron and Cal (Joe Anders and Joseph Zada) vie for the affection of their emotionally absent father. Kazan wrangles with the novel’s central question: Whether those boys can escape the shadows of their family history and forge their own paths—and whether America, carrying the sins of slavery, campaigns of violence against Native Americans, and racism against immigrants building a future in the West, can do the same. Both versions are, in Kazan’s estimation, self-portraits of the artists who made them. Renowned for directing A Streetcar Named Desire and On the Waterfront and co-founding the influential Actors Studio from which the school of “method acting” was born, Elia Kazan was called before the House Un-American Activities Committee in 1952. The Hollywood blacklist was in effect and, Faced with the possibility of never being able to work in Hollywood again, he named names. His granddaughter has never read his autobiography, though she has tried. “There’s some very young part of me that’s like, I still want my grandpa to be my grandpa and not have access to all of this adult stuff,” she says. But she believes that Elia Kazan, who died in 2003 when she was 20, saw himself in the black sheep of the family: “He had this cold and demanding father, and I think he was projecting onto Cal.” Made during a period when the director was facing significant backlash, his version focuses on guilt, rejection, and alienation. Writer Zoe Kazan and her star Florence Pugh on the set of Netflix's East of Eden Geoffrey Short—Netflix What most intrigued Zoe Kazan, a new mother when she started on the script, were characters her grandfather rewrote or omitted: Cathy (Florence Pugh), a mother who walks out on her infant sons and whom Kazan calls “irredeemable, irresistible, and so full of agency,” and Lee (Hoon Lee), a Chinese American servant who steps in to raise the boys. Kazan may live closest to Cathy and Lee in her script. But perhaps the reason she was drawn to the project is the notion of living and working in her grandfather’s shadow. One cannot help but draw the parallel between her and the grandchildren in East of Eden, struggling with whether to embrace or break away from a burdensome legacy. “The irony was not lost on me. There’s a literal way that I’m following in my family’s footsteps,” Kazan admits. “I don’t think I’d be doing my job as an artist if I weren’t thinking about my family while making this family story.” Zoe Kazan was a teenager when she fell hard for Steinbeck. At 15, the Los Angeles native embarked on a Steinbeck-themed road trip up the California coast with her parents, dog-eared East of Eden in hand. Years later, she asked her agents to contact her if anyone purchased the rights. The call came in December 2019, while Kazan was shooting in Australia. She emerged bleary-eyed from a flight back to L.A. with her 15-month-old to pitch her idea to the buyers. “I talked about America and the way that we don’t want to look at our shadow, and how the book argues that embracing your shadow, all of yourself, the good and the bad, is the only way to change,” she says. By then she had built a career not just as an actor in The Big Sick and She Said but as a screenwriter with Ruby Sparks, a satirical take on the manic pixie dream girl, and Wildlife, a drama she co-wrote with her partner, Paul Dano. She landed the East of Eden job and spent the next six years in Steinbeck’s world, and much of that time in the head of one of literature’s most complicated female characters, Cathy. Cathy appears mysteriously on Adam and Charles’ doorstep, badly beaten. From there, she vacillates between a mesmerizing seductress and evil incarnate. She’s a manipulator or a survivor, depending on your perspective. “I don’t think anything in her background excuses her behavior,” says Kazan, laughing. “At the same time, there’s something really compelling about her insistence on her autonomy and selfishness, especially as a woman. I was such a good girl, reading that felt very exciting.” Mike Faist, Christopher Abbott, and Florence Pugh in Netflix's East of Eden Netflix Cathy became the center of Kazan’s adaptation. Often accused by the narrator of being a liar, Cathy in fact often tells the truth. It’s just not a truth the men around her want to hear. She says she does not want to move to California. She says does not want kids. She says she does not want to stay once Cal and Aron are born. Today’s readers may be appalled by the way she earns her freedom, but we can also sympathize with a woman whose wishes are so blatantly ignored. Not even Steinbeck could decide. In letters to his editor, “He starts out like, ‘You’ve never read a monster like this,’” says Kazan. “Then he starts referring to her like, ‘I’ll finally get back to my dear Cathy.’ He seems very seduced by her.” Kazan relied heavily on the book to flesh Cathy out. This is not some girlboss version of one of literature’s great antagonists. “I think the text supports my reading,” she says. “I don’t like historical fiction that imposes modern values on the past. I don’t think Cathy is a feminist. I don’t think she cares about other women. I think she cares about herself.” Pugh was the only actor Kazan named in her script. She’s made an career of morally complex characters in Lady Macbeth, Midsommar, and even the Marvel movies. As Cathy, she’s transfixing. “Florence looks like a doll, but she has this steel rod inside,” says Kazan. “Cathy is all action, like a shark that always has to be moving forward.” East of Eden has aged so well in some respects that Kazan might get accused of making modern updates by those who haven’t read the text. After Cathy leaves Adam, the story centers on two men, Adam and Lee, platonically raising a family and navigating the complicated gender-role implications of that arrangement. Lee’s parents were brought from China to build the railroads. He spends his life being the smartest man in any room but feigning broken English to make the white men around him comfortable, only revealing his intellect to those he trusts. For a few chapters, Lee departs for San Francisco to pursue the dream of opening his own bookstore. He returns with little to say about what happened while he was gone. "Even though [Steinbeck] imagines things like Lee code-switching, which is such a shocking thing to read in this book from 1952, I felt like it was this mystery of what happens to Lee when he goes away,” says Kazan. “I wanted to give him a little bit more." Kazan brought in the novelist Alexander Chee to build out the scenes for Lee that Steinbeck left off the page. She wrote eight episodes, including one exclusively following Lee on his San Francisco excursion. Netflix greenlit seven. "It was the obvious stuff that had to go, partially because it's off the main track of the story, but also because it's all new locations. It would have added a third to our budget,” she says. She had to weave the plot into another episode. “I was so sad to lose that episode, but I do think it's stronger for it, because he's not siloed. He's part of the cohesive story." Hoon Lee in Netflix's East of Eden Netflix When Adam checks out psychologically for the better part of a decade, Lee raises Cal and Aron, deliberately trying to prevent in this new generation the fractures and violence of the previous one. Steinbeck’s narrator observes Lee’s domestic and emotional work with admiration and grants Lee an unexpected dignity given the time period. The Netflix series doubles down, lingering on Lee’s face to capture a flicker of resigned sorrow at moments when Adam claims ownership over the boys and their accomplishments. “A lot of this caretaking, doing the invisible work, my experience of becoming a mother, is embedded in Lee,” Kazan says. But so, too, is she Cathy. “There’s also a part of me, as there is for many mothers, that’s like, ‘I love my children more than anything, and I wish I could disappear.’” Early in our nearly two-hour conversation, Kazan asks a server to lower the music so we can dig our teeth into Lee’s famous monologue on timshel, a Hebrew word that translates to “thou mayest” in the Cain and Abel story. It offers Cain and others the choice to triumph over sin. It frees younger generations from repeating the mistakes of their forebears. One can guess why this may ring true for Zoe Kazan. We circle the question of the divide between an artist and their art as ice melts in her cup. I bring up a tweet she wrote over the summer. When Taylor Swift got married in July and observers clocked the absence of a longtime friend from the wedding, Kazan posted her interpretation of the situation online: "I think she didn't invite her because she has come to the end of her marriage plot, thus cutting off access to her first great subject. Rather than root for her own divorce, she is generating the next chapter of interpersonal drama for herself via female friendship." The tweet read like a literary critic’s interpretation of a pop star’s public art. Swifties called it out of line. Kazan deleted it. "When an artist makes her personal life the subject of her work, and has her wedding in one of our nation's foremost arenas with billboards outside saying ‘Just Married,’ I don't think it's a personal critique to talk about what they're doing in their personal life," she says. "I am very careful to be kind online. I never say anything mean about anyone's work. So I erased those tweets once I realized how upset people were by it. Should I have known better? Probably. But I had never been on the receiving end of Swifties before, so I didn't know." I have spent much of the interview trying to divine how much of East of Eden is an extension of Kazan herself—she cites the timshel passage when referring to her relationship with her family’s legacy. Viewers familiar with her history may theorize about her connection to the material in the same way that Kazan offered up a theory about Swift. “[Swift] puts the names of her exes in her liner notes. There's a way that she has made her private life incredibly public and explicit,” she says. “The overlap with my personal history in this was all very conscious, and made more conscious the longer I worked on it. But it’s not the explicit subject of it.” She hopes the story opens doors inside viewers, the way it did for her. “I think we have tendencies as humans to feel the weight of our nation’s original sins, or our family’s original sins, that they are so shameful and heavy that anything good we do is going to be insignificant,” she says. “And then I think we have this other, very Trumpian impulse, of whitewashing the past. I think the thing the book is arguing for, and that I find very moving, is this idea that we actually have to carry both things. The weight of our original sins, and the idea that we are not defined by them.”

In PBS's Heartrending Trespasses, Love Defies War in '70s Belfast

TIME | 2026-09-18
