Update your preferences in Account Settings Tom Cullen and Lola Petticrew in Trespasses Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In an early scene of the exquisitely devastating miniseries Trespasses, two strangers in a pub ruminate on the relationship between art and pain. Cushla (Lola Petticrew), a schoolteacher taking a shift behind her family’s bar, admits she couldn’t finish reading A Clockwork Orange; the knowledge that the novel’s author, Anthony Burgess, was inspired by soldiers’ savage attack on his wife disturbed her too much. “He turned his suffering into art,” protests her customer, Michael (Tom Cullen). “I’d rather have the art without the suffering,” she retorts. “I’m sure his wife would, too.” Michael isn’t quite swayed. “Can you have one without the other?” he wonders. Such beer-in-hand debates are as old as public drunkenness, but the conversation takes on new layers as the Troubles rage and Cushla and Michael’s romance blossoms in mid-1970s Belfast. She’s an empathetic Catholic, caring for a hypercritical mom (Gillian Anderson’s Gina) who has been lost in alcoholism since the death of her saintly husband and trying to help a young student who’s being bullied by the religious school’s leaders as well as his peers about his own parents’ “mixed” marriage. A Protestant barrister who speaks out against brutality by loyalist police, Michael has made fierce enemies on both sides of the conflict. All around the nascent couple is evidence that their love is doomed. Yet, like so many star-crossed lovers of fiction, they are drawn together by a force stronger than the ones pushing them apart. Whether you see their story, which debuted on Channel 4 in the UK and will premiere Sept. 20 on PBS, as a triumph or as a tragedy will depend on whose view of suffering you find most convincing. Lola Petticrew, left, and Gillian Anderson in Trespasses Wildgaze Films and All3Media International Adapted by Ailbhe Keogan (Bad Sisters) from Louise Kennedy’s celebrated 2022 novel, Trespasses defies Romeo and Juliet cliché through specificity. It is a profound geopolitical injustice that the past few generations of Westerners mostly know war, even when it’s initiated by our own governments, as a misfortune that befalls people thousands of miles away, in dusty deserts and steaming jungles. Keogan subverts that notion, depicting Belfast as a war zone with violence at every doorstep and hatred festering on every corner. Everyone knows which side their neighbors are on—and surveils them, consciously or not, to make sure they stay on it. The characters are no mere victims of circumstance. Petticrew, fresh off a thrillingly dark turn in Furious and returning to the place and era of her breakthrough role in the IRA drama Say Nothing, gives us a protagonist who is as bright and stubborn as she is compassionate. You can see why she’d fall for an older, more mature man over the boyish types who clumsily court her. Cullen makes his character irresistible—a crusader who voices unpopular truths, warning law enforcement that their cruelty only mints new IRA recruits. He’s not a pure hero, though. He faces his minefield of a life by compartmentalizing; Cushla knows he’s married, but his infidelity is not up for discussion. The slumped and slurring Gina seems, at first, to be the kind of messy grande dame Anderson has taken to playing in recent years, with mixed results. But even this broad matriarch deepens, and the performance hits subtler notes, in later episodes. Just about every character surprises us, at least once, for good or ill. That complexity serves a thematic purpose as well as a narrative one. War flattens the enemy to slurs and stereotypes. Fighting for humanity means insisting upon individual identity; it means believing a Catholic and a Protestant in ’70s Belfast might belong together. “We must find the bravery to choose freedom over fear,” Michael proclaims in one of his righteous moments. “We cannot let the old bigots and the boys with guns tell us how to live.” For a couple on opposite sides of a war that allows for no gray area, this may be a suicidal ideology. But even if art is possible in the absence of suffering, love never comes without the risk of heartbreak. Whether it justifies that pain, Trespasses suggests, is a question too fraught and personal to be resolved by any glib barroom debate.

The Earthshot Prize Is Embracing the Idea of Positive Tipping Points
2026-09-18

The Earthshot Prize Is Embracing the Idea of Positive Tipping Points

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings At a time when companies are racing to deliver commercial fusion energy and promising a new generation of geothermal wells across the U.S., three-wheel electric vehicles may not seem like the most exciting clean energy technology. But in India, three-wheel vehicles are an essential part of daily transportation and account for up to a quarter of motorized trips in many cities. The majority of new full-size passenger auto rickshaws sold in India are now electric, according to registration data, a striking milestone given that 10 years ago they were barely off the ground. Initially, scaling took a mix of policy, infrastructure build out, and innovative financing; now, the market has taken on a life of its own. “Once you cross 50%, electric becomes the dominant technology,” says Suman Mishra, the CEO of Mahindra Last Mile Mobility, a key player in the market. The development underscores an idea that is gaining currency in some climate circles: sometimes technologies that matter most are not the futuristic breakthroughs waiting to scale but the familiar ones where adoption begins to reinforce itself. That shift sits at the heart of a new framework the Earthshot Prize is using this year to evaluate climate solutions. Rather than simply picking the climate solution with the most impressive technology, the judges looked for ways to recognize and advance “positive tipping points” that will unlock transformative changes beyond any individual company or project. “We know exactly the things that we’re looking for to try to tip these global systems in the right direction,” says Jason Knauf, CEO of the Earthshot Prize. The pattern is visible across the finalist list. Mahindra Last Mile Mobility, one of this year’s Earthshot finalists announced on Sept. 18, has not just built affordable electric three-wheelers but also helped facilitate the infrastructure and policy necessary for them to take off. Another finalist, the Zero Emission Maritime Buyers Alliance, or ZEMBA for short, has sought to coordinate demand for low-carbon shipping fuel from customers that ship goods. And Yellowstone to Yukon restores habitat corridors across North America, which then unlocks feedbacks that restore the underlying ecological ecosystem. The concept of climate tipping points entered the vernacular nearly two decades ago to describe some of the most alarming risks posed by climate change. At the time, tipping points referred to the thresholds that, when crossed, would trigger a chain reaction of often irreversible damage—think of Antarctic ice sheet collapse leading to sea level rise or the destruction of the Amazon releasing stored carbon dioxide. More recently, scientists have sought to reframe tipping points, showing that positive developments can have tipping points, too. Cheap energy leads to a rewiring of economies. Social movements unlock widespread policy changes. “The tipping point is where you have strong amplification within the system,” says Tim Lenton, a professor of the University of Exeter who has written seminal papers on the tipping points concept. The reframe comes at an important moment for the climate narrative. With increasingly bleak climate news, positive tipping points offer grounded optimism about how things may change. That was a key logic for Earthshot’s embrace of tipping points. “We don’t think you can just leave people with the negatives and the warnings,” says Knauf. “We think we have a responsibility to show them” where positive action is possible. But the positive tipping point framework is also useful from a capital allocation perspective. As companies cut costs and governments look for ways to trim public spending, tipping points offer a way to identify what Lenton describes as the best “bang for your buck” for climate investment dollars. In some cases, that means supporting initiatives outside the typical wheelhouse of climate technology startups. ZEMBA, for example, works with the customers who would like to reduce their shipping footprint with low-carbon fuel but cannot afford to do so on their own, thereby helping create a demand signal. That’s a coordinated effort that competitors rarely engage in. “Once those markets are spinning, we will fade into the mist,” says Ingrid Irigoyen, the organization’s CEO. Such an approach makes sense for governments evaluating how they spend their public dollars. It also makes sense for pension funds and institutional investors concerned with the long-term economic implications of climate change. With relatively few dollars, certain investments will generate a long-tail of positive climate outcomes. In a future world where climate impact is an important investment metric, tipping points may be a useful tool. For those interested, the Earthshot finalists may offer a useful place to start.

When George W. Bush Defended Muslim Americans
2026-09-18

When George W. Bush Defended Muslim Americans

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
On September 17, 2001, George W. Bush visited the Islamic Center of Washington, D.C. Six days had passed since the 9/11 attacks, and in that time, reports of harassment and violence against Muslim Americans had surged. In Salt Lake City, a man attempted to burn down a Pakistani American restaurant. Near Phoenix, a gunman murdered a Sikh man he thought was Muslim. Community leaders at the center told Bush their stories that morning. The president listened. Then, in front of the mosque’s intricate tiles and graceful arches, the country’s most prominent Christian politician spoke. He addressed the terrorist attacks: “These acts of violence against innocents violate the fundamental tenets of the Islamic faith.” Citing a passage from the Quran, Bush described Islam as a religion in which “billions of people find comfort and solace and peace.” He then discussed Muslims’ being targeted, stressing that people “who feel like they can intimidate our fellow citizens to take out their anger don’t represent the best of America—they represent the worst of humankind.” Bush’s message at the Islamic Center, which he echoed numerous times in post-9/11 remarks, now feels like a remnant from a bygone era. As my colleague Jonathan Chait recently argued, antagonistic, vicious rhetoric about Islam has become more and more widespread on the right. Donald Trump said in the Oval Office last month, in reference to the upcoming midterm elections, that “we have jihadists being elected all over the place.” Also last month, Representative Nancy Mace of South Carolina wrote on social media that “every single Muslim holding public office in America” is “a threat to both national security and our republic.” When asked about her comment, Mace replied, “I don’t want to hear the call to prayer five times a day in an American city. I think it’s wrong; it’s not our values.” Read: The man who refused to sit on the sidelines Many Republican elected officials have cast their vilification of Islam as a defense of Christianity. “Muslims don’t belong in American society,” Representative Andy Ogles of Tennessee posted on social media in March; the next day, he decried the “importation of Islam” because “Christianity is the answer. Always.” In a June statement, Representative Keith Self of Texas said that “Christian values form the foundation of our nation” and described Sharia—a set of religious guidelines for Muslims—as “wholly incompatible with the rule of law in the United States.” Some people on the right have made Bush himself an object of scorn for the way he spoke about Islam after 9/11. The social-media account End Wokeness—which frequently complains that contemporary America is inhospitable to Christians—recently attacked him for delivering his remarks at the Islamic Center while “they were still digging bodies.” Last fall, the far-right provocateur Laura Loomer said on her Rumble show that Bush’s rhetoric “set our country up for the last 24 years of Islamic infiltration.” Her guest, Representative Chip Roy of Texas, nodded in agreement and bemoaned “the wussification” of his fellow Republicans, who he said had come to share a “false” belief that America isn’t a Christian nation. “We let that take root. People believed it, promoted it, and pushed it, so that everybody, like, got in the corner and, like, ‘Oh, we can’t talk about religion.’” An implication of Roy and Loomer’s exchange, and of much of the alarmist rhetoric on the right, is that tolerance of Islam must stem from a watered-down understanding of Christianity. Being a Christian, in this view, demands boldly doing away with the niceties of pluralism. That may be a more popular view on the right than it was when Bush spoke at the Islamic Center 25 years ago, but it is spiritually vapid. Christianity does not require antagonism toward Muslims—in fact, many theologians argue that Christians are called to treat Islam with respect. The anti-Islam, ostensibly pro-Christianity rhetoric on the right today gets a fundamental tenet of the faith wrong, according to theologians I spoke with. “As Christians, we believe that all human beings are image-bearers of God,” Matthew Kaemingk, a professor at Theological University Utrecht who studies Christian-Muslim relations, and is an evangelical Christian, told me. “And so Muslims are beloved by God, regardless of theological beliefs that they have that I deeply disagree with.” When he surveys scripture—the Book of Isaiah’s description of kin and foreigner worshipping God, for example, or the New Testament account of Jesus’s apostles speaking in many Near Eastern tongues during Pentecost—Kaemingk sees a God who doesn’t recoil from cultural difference but rejoices in it. In this light, he said, scapegoating and demonization of Muslims is both unjust and “blasphemous.” Kaemingk is not a theological liberal—he holds this view of Islam while also having a traditional understanding of the Christian faith. Islam and Christianity aren’t interchangeable to him; not all religions are the same. As is the case with many evangelical Christians, he believes that accepting Jesus Christ is the only path to salvation. Nevertheless, he has written that Christians can “defend the rights, dignity, and humanity of their Muslim neighbors without downplaying their exclusive commitment to Christian orthodoxy.” Some strains of Christianity see God at work in Islam. In two documents from the Second Vatican Council, the Catholic Church clarified its stance that God’s “plan of salvation” includes Muslims, “who, professing to hold the faith of Abraham, along with us adore the one and merciful God,” and declared that it holds Islam “with esteem.” Jordan Denari Duffner, a Catholic theologian and scholar in residence for the interfaith Shoulder to Shoulder Campaign, told me that the Catholic disposition toward Islam “is not even one of neutrality, but is one of positivity.” In her 2017 book on Catholicism and Islam, Duffner points out similarities between the two faiths: their shared devotion to Jesus’s mother, Mary, for example, and prayers that seem to echo each other. In the 13th century, Saint Francis of Assisi visited the Muslim sultan Malik al-Kamil in Egypt. Soon after, Francis composed “The Praises of God,” a prayer that resembles the Muslim litany of divine invocations called the dhikr. Hostility toward Islam from Christians on the American right isn’t new. After 9/11, figures such as the televangelist Pat Robertson urged Bush to characterize Islam as inherently militaristic. The president didn’t listen, but over time, Robertson’s view became more prominent. The 2016 Republican presidential primary was a turning point: Some candidates decried “radical Islamic terrorism,” or some variant of the phrase, which linked Islam to violence but did not go so far as to say that the religion as a whole was a threat. “Politicians could say, We’re not talking about all Muslims here; we are just talking about, you know, the dangerous ones,” Duffner told me. But Donald Trump targeted Muslims as a group—and was elected president nonetheless. One of the very first things he did upon taking office in 2017 was issue his “Muslim ban” executive order, limiting travel and immigration from seven Muslim-majority countries. Anti-Muslim rhetoric has only gotten broader and more overt in the years since. As Religion News Service recently reported, Andy Ogles and Nancy Mace reflect a growing tendency on the right to treat Islam writ large as “a civilizational threat.” In an interview last week reflecting on the 25th anniversary of 9/11, Bush was asked why he’d decided to visit the Islamic Center. He said that he’d been concerned about stories of women in head coverings “being harassed at shopping centers” and that he believed “one of the most important parts of our ideology” is “the freedom to worship.” He didn’t explicitly invoke—let alone champion—Christianity. But his words reflected a far more authentically Christian posture toward Islam than the one spouted by many of today’s supposed defenders of the faith.

Neil Young’s Epic Reckoning With Mortality
2026-09-18

Neil Young’s Epic Reckoning With Mortality

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
At first listen, Neil Young’s new song “Soon I Might Be Going” comes across like a sad shake of the head at the hot and Trumpy 2020s. Electric guitars fizzle with the slow majesty of a dying campfire; Young’s rooster-crow voice sounds tired and hoarse. He sings of a president on TV tearing down the old White House. He describes a Christmas in the Rockies that’s warm as summer, during which “the clouds that used to pour the rain / Are billowy and strange.” He notices new cars on the street, new buildings in his town, and feels like reality is starting to resemble fiction. But this is not a lament for the present—or at least not only that. One line references a “new” movie, The Crimson Pirate, which was released in 1952, when Young was about 7 years old. Back then, the president on TV would have been Harry Truman, who oversaw his own gut renovation of the White House. The 80-year-old Young could be remembering a warm Christmas from childhood. He’s cataloging similarities between his early days and now—including a sense that a “revolution’s coming / Like a fighter with a fist.” Young himself is an icon of a revolution that pitted the Baby Boomers against their elders—but all along, he was more interested in the links across generations. In a voice blending choir-boy innocence and weathered-cowboy experience, he captured feelings of recognition between the young man and the old man, the child and the parent, the modern and the historical. His discography, filled with sequels and callbacks, has moved between folk quietude and rockin’ raggedness in a manner that’s felt less like the progression of a story than the cycling of seasons. As he once famously said onstage, his music is “all one song.” Now his latest studio album, Second Song, offers a particularly moving experience of time travel. At a time when many of his contemporaries have slowed their output, Young’s maintained teenage restlessness. The past 10 years have brought at least 13 new albums—some with freshly written tracks, some with unearthed ones, some with live recordings—as well as a number of reissues and nonmusical projects. He’s stayed in the news by taking important stances, like by pulling his music from Spotify for two years to protest its platforming of Joe Rogan, while maintaining an online newspaper called NYA Times Contrarian (recent headline: “Elon Musk—Among the Most Evil of the Billionaires”). Hard-core fans have kept up, but the magic of his late period is that it’s happening regardless of who’s paying attention. One might, at any point, dip in and find a banging rerecording of a proto-grunge masterpiece, a book about high-quality audio technology, or an okay song about the misdeeds of white guys. But Young’s new album—his second studio effort with the Chrome Hearts, the band he formed in 2024 when his longtime group, Crazy Horse, went on break—feels like a minor landmark. Maybe that’s because it addresses mortality in a way that feels stark for an artist who paused touring earlier this year because he was, he said, “listening to my body” (he’s performing again now). Or maybe it’s because the album seems unusually coherent. Young has stayed prolific because of what my colleague James Parker once called “a highly evolved and very disciplined aesthetic of half-assedness.” (Young himself has renounced some of his most classic lyrics as “gibberish.”) The first Chrome Hearts album, 2025’s Talkin to the Trees, was a variety pack of short, noisy sketches. Second Song is different: gentle instrumentation, lengthy runtimes, unified purpose. On “Day After Day,” he explains, “There’s something I want to say and say it slow.” Read: What happens when a beloved rock star gets fed up? On the opener, “The Foggy Edge of Time,” a piano’s steady pulse mixes with guitar licks that seem to be playing backwards, creating the sense of a reverie unfolding. Young tours through his life, juxtaposing highlights (like the birth of his three kids) with “bumps along the way.” Then he arrives at a refrain that’s sung a bit funny: “alcohol / alcohol.” The effect is to wryfully brighten what might seem like a misty and sentimental exercise; throughout the album, stunning observations are mixed with goofy or plainly underwritten ones. Young’s famously loose, improvisatory creative approach (or, yes, half-assedness) is one of the things he’s examining: On “Second Song,” he wakes up with “a melody and a rhyme” in his head, adding, “The words just tumbled forward.” Still, the scale of that tumbling is epic. The seven-minute-long “Day After Day” is a catalog of ordinary occasions: making coffee, listening to the drips of melting snow, hanging out with his wife, Daryl Hannah. An acoustic riff drives and an organ hums while the rhythm section keeps time not quite steadily, sometimes slipping behind. Listening crystallizes the way that life’s monotony can be a blessing. As the song’s banalities pile up, so does the realization that sooner or later, the routine will have to end. He makes that thought explicit over the nine minutes of “Soon I Might Be Going,” in which the resemblance between the past and present serves as reassurance that “if I’m not here to see it / Doesn’t mean it’s gone away.” But for Young, always the protest rocker, acceptance of time’s passage is not the same as shrugging at it. On the title track (more than 11 minutes long), he describes young generations denied the optimism that their parents held at their age. “Too much has changed around us / But we can bring it back somehow,” Young sings, stretching and shaking the last syllable. In addition to these voyages through memory—and one short paean to Hannah—the album features two rerecordings of songs he wrote early in his career, before achieving stardom. Sung with his teenage band the Squires, the 1964 track “I’ll Love You Forever” was a gooey Beach Boys homage; the new version is a frayed country song with a touch of tropical sparkle. As Young vows devotion to a lover for eternity—a vow that signifies something a bit different at his age than it did when he first wrote it—he brings out the way that the march of decades can change the meaning of even pop’s simplest lyrical tropes. But what’s more astonishing is how the song hasn’t changed—how across eras, across all sorts of sounds, the same brain has been at work, pondering the mystery of forever.

Jon Ossoff Is Doing Something Different
2026-09-18

Jon Ossoff Is Doing Something Different

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
If you’re a Democrat, attending a Jon Ossoff rally feels very good. Therapeutic, even. Many Democrats feel as if every day of the second Trump administration has brought some fresh horror: a discouraging development in the never-ending Iran war; a new vow of retribution from the 80-year-old leader of the free world; or the revelation of yet another money-making venture cooked up by the Trumps. But now, from the South, a young senator has emerged, with a deep voice promising change and a speaking cadence not unlike Barack Obama’s. His dark hair sits just so; his skin is perfectly even—is that makeup he’s wearing? Never mind. We’re looking at his sleeves now, which are carefully rolled up and emphasize biceps that are, well, quite noticeably larger than they were when Georgia voters sent him to Congress in 2021. More important, this senator sounds different from other Democrats. Ossoff is doing something on the campaign trail that basically no one else in his party seems able to pull off: elegantly and comprehensively skewering the Trump administration, while earning some Republican support. He’s doing this in speeches at his campaign events, such as the one I attended on Sunday in Athens, Georgia. But those speeches seem carefully calibrated to be heard by an audience much farther away: Every week, millions of Americans are encountering Ossoff on their phones, through perfectly produced clips of the senator unveiling a new insult or innovative critique of the Trump administration. “He’s trying to put his face on the money. Did you see that? He’s building a monument to himself,” he said in May. “He’s doing these things now because no one will honor him when he’s gone.” Last month, the senator’s reference to the zealous Trump aide Natalie Harp spiked Google searches for Harp by 5,000 percent. The revelation of Ossoff’s particular talent explains why the senator is suddenly all over the news and Instagram, and why so many people in his party are breathlessly positioning him as the new favorite to win the Democratic nomination in 2028. Premature? Maybe. Ossoff might not be well suited for such a task. But Democrats are desperate for leaders and feel that the senator has pierced the party’s gloom like a beam of warm light. (Ossoff, through a spokesperson, declined an interview for this story.) Outside the Georgia Theatre in Athens, Judith McWillie struggled to explain how she felt about her senator. The retired professor sighed, blinked, and sighed some more, like a computer malfunctioning after being assigned too many tasks. But then, at last, she smiled a dreamy smile. “It’s like taking a clean shower to hear these things spoken so clearly,” she told me. She hadn’t even gotten to see the senator in person—the rally was so packed that she’d had to watch on a screen from the taqueria next door. Disappointing. But she’d seen what she needed to see. “I’ve been waiting for the Democrats to do that for 15 years.” Ossoff is expected to win his reelection bid in November against his Republican opponent. But it’s still not clear that the senator would be able to go beyond scripted zingers if he decides to campaign for higher office. It’s obvious at an Ossoff event that the senator once worked as a documentary filmmaker. His rallies have an almost cinematic quality—professional lighting, well-dispersed signs with stylish fonts and vivid colors, plenty of American-flag bunting. Every clip the campaign releases after a rally is perfectly cropped for social media, and some photos are angled upward to show the senator looking out over the crowd, his sharp jawline front and center. (This framing has the added benefit of being recyclable for another, perhaps higher-profile, campaign down the line.) In Athens, the press risers were, much to the chagrin of some attendees, right in the middle of the crowd. Ossoff himself stood on a part of the stage that jutted toward the audience. Behind him, his team had assembled a diverse display of good-looking young people. During his speech, the crowd was treated to language equal parts poetic and biting. On Iran: “We sink in the quicksand of reckless war.” On President Trump: “Our task is not just to contain his wickedness but to cure the rot that gave rise to it.” The current president is “the broken old man,” and Ossoff’s opponent, Republican Representative Mike Collins, is “a crumbling little bigot.” In a few short lines, Ossoff managed to squeeze in virtually every Democratic critique of Trump and his administration: “They doubled your insurance premiums and slashed Medicaid to cut taxes for their corporate donors and the ultrarich. They made you pay more for everything. They lied us into war and spent most of the last year covering up for the world’s most famous pedophile.” Ossoff is eager to highlight and explain to voters the Trump family’s alleged state-sponsored enrichment schemes, and concluded for the Athens audience that “it pays to be a Trump, especially when the whole world knows Daddy’s a crook.” Days after a former Anthropic researcher warned that artificial intelligence could wipe out humanity, Ossoff introduced a new line on AI, arguing that Trump has too many investments with tech companies to ever regulate them. “A capable president would move swiftly to secure and reassure the American people, rush inspectors into frontier labs, fortify the nation against bioterrorism,” he said. “But President Trump is compromised.” Read: The only thing capable of uniting Bernie and Bannon During Ossoff’s speeches, he lays out the several endorsements he’s received from Georgia Republicans who commend his office’s constituent services. Then, in a call for national unity, he hits the crowd with an “e pluribus unum,” and everyone goes bananas. Some progressives believe that Ossoff is too moderate—or worse, too generic. They argue that his appeal does not go much further than his generally pleasing aura. In fact, I have never attended a political rally where a candidate’s supporters seemed quite so unable to articulate a reason for their support. A couple of attendees cited Ossoff’s push for affordable insulin and his work preventing child trafficking. But mostly, their compliments were vague. “I couldn’t specify anything,” one woman told me before the rally, even though she’d arrived early enough to press right up against the barricade. “He votes the way I want him to,” another person said. This changed after Ossoff finished speaking, when people sounded totally stunned. “He’s gonna be a star!” one woman exclaimed to her friend as they left the theater. Other attendees used words such as inspiring, articulate, and refreshing. Older voters heard the echoes of another well-spoken young Democrat. “Jon has the charisma that Kennedy had,” Susan Cartwright, a retiree from Athens, told me. Ossoff events are a warm bath for Democrats because they represent exactly what they’ve been demanding since Trump’s reelection: a leader who demonstrates some mettle and who, by virtue of his youth, can communicate a vigorous, anti-Trump message using modern methods. Other Democrats “will take a term paper to a gun fight,” Fred Hicks, a Democratic strategist based in Georgia, told me. But Ossoff “is able to position things in sound bites and make complex ideas very simple.” His looks also seem to help. “He’s gorgeous,” one elderly woman told me at the rally, adding, conspiratorially, “You know some people are calling him ‘Senator My Boo.’” The task of stopping Senator My Boo has fallen to Mike Collins, but the situation doesn’t look promising for the Republican nominee. It’s shaping up to be a good year nationally for Democrats, and in Georgia, Ossoff has the advantage of incumbency. Collins is also facing a triumvirate of controversies that would be bad enough on their own but together amount to campaign poison: A former staffer for Collins posted a swastika and a photo of himself in a Ku Klux Klan–style robe on social media. Collins’s son-in-law, a social-media influencer, has shared white-nationalist rhetoric and anti-Semitic conspiracy theories. To top it off, the House Ethics Committee is investigating Collins for allegedly misusing taxpayer funds. “Mike Collins is going to get his teeth kicked in; it’s just a matter of how many teeth,” a state Republican consultant, who agreed to speak on the condition of anonymity, told me. “There’s nobody here who thinks he has a chance.” Meyer Siegfried, a spokesperson for the Collins campaign, dismissed this analysis as “a fantasy pushed by anonymous operatives captured by the Ossoff hype machine.” Georgia, he added, “remains a serious battleground.” It seems obvious that Ossoff isn’t merely hoping to beat Collins. He’s hoping to win so big that his tide lifts all the other Democratic boats in Georgia—with the helpful side effect of demonstrating to his party that he enjoys broad appeal in a state that voted for Trump in 2024. Some Democrats are predicting—or perhaps manifesting—that if he wins in November, Ossoff will turn immediately toward a bigger project: running for president in 2028. Ossoff has repeatedly denied that he is interested in doing this. If he won, he’d have to give up his hard-fought Senate seat, which, if Georgia elects a Republican governor this year, would be politically perilous for Democrats. Ossoff, who has two daughters under the age of 5, is also very protective of his family life. But national Democrats don’t care much about these caveats, and many consultants can’t wait to draft him into the 2028 race—although none wanted to engage in such speculation on the record. One referred to him as an “S-tier” presidential candidate. On the prediction-market platform Kalshi, Ossoff’s odds of winning the 2028 nomination are higher than any Democrat’s. The people at the Athens rally were certainly keen on the possibility.  Similarly pugnacious candidates, such as Gavin Newsom, “have a lot of negativity that follows them around,” Cartwright said. But Ossoff “would be one of the best choices we could have.” Deborah Dykstra, a 68-year-old from Athens, slapped my arm excitedly when I suggested that her senator might run for president. “He’s young; he’s intelligent,” she said. “I heard him speak when Kamala was in Atlanta, and I liked listening to what he was saying better.” Read: Gavin Newsom would rather be wrong than weak It’s not entirely clear that Ossoff would be good at running for president. Like Obama, the Georgia senator is confident onstage reading from a teleprompter. Unlike Obama, Ossoff has yet to prove that he’s entirely comfortable speaking off-the-cuff. In interviews, he rarely seems willing to let down his guard. Asked for his favorite swear word in a conversation with The Bulwark’s Tim Miller, who tends toward playful interviews, Ossoff would not be drawn in. “That’s just one of those private questions,” Ossoff said. The man is consistent. In a 2012 tweet, Ossoff wrote that he’d thought twice about sharing an explicit song because “I don’t want someone to ask why I linked to profanity in 15 yrs.” He similarly avoids playing along when TMZ reporters ask silly questions on Capitol Hill. You might call it being relentlessly on-message. “He’s always been serious,” Hicks, the Democratic strategist, said. “He would say the moment we’re in requires a little gravity.” But, as the former Obama adviser David Axelrod once said, presidential campaigns “are like an MRI for the soul.” They require something more than straightforward speech-making: an ability to sit down with Joe Rogan for a three-hour, stream-of-consciousness interview, for example. Or, as the old political adage goes, an unbuttoned ease that makes voters want to pull up beside them at the bar. Perhaps Jon Ossoff has yet to fully reveal himself. His supporters in Georgia predict that he will. “I get the feeling, too, that there’s a lot more even coming from Ossoff,” McWillie, the retired professor, said. “He’s doing it carefully, in stages, instead of trying to just throw everything out there all at once.” The 2028 presidential primary could hinge on whether the senator learns how to go off script.
The Atlantic
RFK Jr.’s Brazen Reassurance to Anti-Vaxxers
2026-09-17

RFK Jr.’s Brazen Reassurance to Anti-Vaxxers

The Atlantic | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Since becoming Health and Human Services secretary, Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has repeatedly denied that he opposes vaccines and has toned down some of his more extreme rhetoric on the subject. But in a keynote speech today at a conference for Children’s Health Defense, the anti-vaccine organization he founded, the old Kennedy was back. He complained about safety studies, floated disproven theories about a link between vaccines and autism, and all but declared himself an anti-vaxxer.  “You have a strong and steadfast friend at the White House,” he told the crowd. His choice to take the stage at the conference signaled a shift. Since becoming HHS secretary, Kennedy had distanced himself from Children’s Health Defense as he worked to convince the Senate that he held mainstream-enough views on vaccines. Children’s Health Defense, meanwhile, attempts to convince parents that vaccines are a threat to their children’s well-being. In just the past few days, CHD has used its X account to warn parents against trusting babies with health-care providers who might give them vaccines, claimed that childhood vaccines kill more children than do the diseases they prevent, and alleged that COVID vaccines were designed to cause infertility. The speakers at its conference were a who’s who of the anti-vaccine movement. Nearly everything the organization promotes runs counter to guidance from the CDC, an agency that Kennedy leads as head of the Department of Health and Human Services. But much of Kennedy’s speech was consistent with CHD’s brand of scaremongering. He suggested that vaccines contribute to chronic disease and disorders such as autism. He bemoaned the fact that more vaccines are recommended for American children now than when he was a child. And he falsely claimed that nothing is known about their safety. “We have no idea what the risk profile is for these products,” he told the crowd. In one of the more bizarre moments of his address, Kennedy read from the manufacturer’s list of possible adverse events for the vaccine against hepatitis B, a disease that can lead to liver failure. Among those he mentioned was anaphylaxis, an allergic reaction that occurs roughly once for every 1 million doses administered. He ignored the benefits of the vaccine, which has drastically reduced infections and is projected to have prevented millions of deaths. Instead, he implied that the vaccine was merely part of an industry “gold rush.” The speakers surrounding Kennedy are publicly hostile to vaccination. He was introduced by Mary Holland, the CEO of Children’s Health Defense, who took over the organization after Kennedy’s departure and praised him for taking on “the corrupt medical pharmaceutical complex.” Mark Gorton, the president of a pro-Kennedy think tank called the MAHA Institute, had earlier told the crowd that vaccines are a “public-health nightmare” and that eliminating them would make the country healthier. And then there was Andrew Wakefield, known for his discredited 1998 Lancet paper that alleged a connection between autism and the MMR vaccine. Anti-vaccine activists have spent decades citing Wakefield’s faulty research in an effort to fuel fear of immunizations. Now is a terrible time for Kennedy to return to public anti-vaccine advocacy. Pennsylvania is in the midst of a deadly measles outbreak. The official count in the state is 731 cases since April and 117 in just the past week; the real number of infections is almost certainly much higher. Four people, two of them infants, have died since mid-August. Much of the suffering has been concentrated among the state’s Amish community, where immunization rates are low, about 25 percent in Lancaster County. Before becoming HHS secretary, Kennedy praised the Amish for declining to get vaccinated, telling a crowd at an Amish country fair in 2021 that, when it comes to vaccines, “it’s so important for us to think more like the Amish think.” Rather than speaking publicly about how low vaccination rates are the reason that measles is sickening and killing Americans, Kennedy has spent the past month squabbling with Pennsylvania’s governor, Josh Shapiro, over how to define measles-associated deaths. Under Kennedy’s leadership, the CDC has so far declined to list any deaths from Pennsylvania’s ongoing outbreak; this afternoon, while he was onstage, HHS’s official X account called the state government’s demands that the CDC do so “political blackmail.” In his speech at the conference, Kennedy’s only mention of measles was part of a complaint that deaths from the disease “get headlines” while the alleged dangers of vaccines are ignored. (An HHS spokesperson didn’t respond to a question about why Kennedy was appearing at an anti-vaccine conference during a measles outbreak.) Kennedy reassured the crowd that research into vaccine safety was going on behind the scenes at HHS. “As I said, you have friends in the White House,” he said, “and we are doing this one day at a time.” He claimed that HHS had failed to study vaccine risks and told the audience that he was remedying that. He mentioned making use of the Vaccine Safety Datalink, a collection of Americans’ electronic health records. That database has long been a topic of fascination for Kennedy and other anti-vaccine activists who believe that it might contain hidden evidence that vaccines are harmful. Kennedy built his reputation on being the country’s most prominent anti-vaccine activist. As HHS secretary, he has often emphasized less controversial issues, such as ultraprocessed foods and artificial dyes. Today, he took to the stage with a message for his longtime allies: He is still one of them.
The Atlantic
Buttigieg: Democrats Need to Get Serious About Amending the Constitution
2026-09-17

Buttigieg: Democrats Need to Get Serious About Amending the Constitution

The Atlantic | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
In a conversation at The Atlantic Festival with The Atlantic’s editor in chief, Jeffrey Goldberg, former Secretary of Transportation Pete Buttigieg warned fellow members of the Democratic Party to resist the urge to revert to “the previous status quo” if they regain power in the midterms. It would be a mistake, Buttigieg argued, for Democrats to try to reverse “the damage” of the past two years of the Donald Trump presidency by trying to restore the country to how it looked in 2022 or “whenever we think that America was great.” He said that Democrats instead need to build for the idea that “the next quarter century between here and 2050, which is kind of a make-or-break quarter century for the whole American project, is subject to very different conditions and problems than the ones that we inherited.” Buttigieg—whom many analysts see as a potential 2028 presidential contender—offered examples of what that vision might look like. He called for banning gerrymandering and passing a constitutional amendment to address the deluge of money in politics. “This idea that it is somehow beyond our power to use what is probably the single most elegant feature of the Constitution, which is its capacity to be amended—right?—is a complete failure of ambition and imagination,” Buttigieg said. When asked by Goldberg about the Democratic Socialists of America, Buttigieg responded that he thinks “there’s a temptation to imagine that being bold is the same thing as being kind of maximally ideological.” Sometimes it is the case, Buttigieg said, that the leftmost idea is the boldest. But other, more ideologically moderate projects may require “radical reform to our institutions,” he noted. “I know the commentariat wants to get it all fixed on some left-to-center-to-right spectrum,” Buttigieg continued. “To me, what’s more interesting is: Do we have the right package of base hits and big swings, and are we going after both of them, at the same time, all the time?” The following is an edited transcript of the conversation. Jeffrey Goldberg: I want to get to the state of our civilization, but there’s this thing—and we’ve talked about this already—there’s this thing that’s been bothering me more than most things bothering me in the past few days. The fact that a Vladimir Putin–aligned oligarch, extremely dubious past—as opposed to the oligarchs with a wholesome past— Extremely dubious past, funded openly—or not openly, it was discovered, but there’s no secret—funded a two-day celebration for the president of the United States’ son. And we were talking a little bit about how that seems fairly normal in our new reality, but if you actually step back a few years ago, in a Bush presidency, Obama presidency, and told somebody, Oh, yeah, the president of the United States’ kid’s wedding is being underwritten by a Russian oligarch, no one would believe that we’ve entered that level of dystopic reality. So my question is: How screwed are we? Pete Buttigieg: Well, if I thought we were altogether screwed, I wouldn’t be doing this. Goldberg: What would you be doing? Moving to Canada? Goldberg: I just want to know; he’s got access to different forms of transportation. Buttigieg: Is that what giving up looks like now? Moving to Canada? No, I think it’s symptomatic of this bigger pattern we’ve been in where we stare down something that is completely unacceptable and then just move on. And in some ways, the big thing, the spectacular corruption, actually is harder to face. It’s like if you’re in Washington, D.C., and you walk up to the Washington Monument and you stick your hand on it and you look up—which, by the way, is a fascinating experience if you ever get the chance, because you can walk right up to it—but once you do that, you cannot make out the shape or even the size of the Washington Monument, right? Because it’s right there. And when we have monumental corruption or monumental systemic breakdowns, I think it can have the same effect. Part of what I’m trying to do, as I travel the country, is insist that we’ve got to deal with a whole set of things that we’ve just been talking about being wrong for a long time. I’m not just talking about the near term, the recent shocking, spectacular forms of corruption, like what you mentioned about the wedding or the sentences that are coming out of our mouth, right? Half a billion dollars of crypto money from a foreign prince going to the first family, or the jumbo jet—I mean, we all know this list of things, right? Goldberg: Or renaming a cultural center named after a martyred president for yourself. Buttigieg: For example, and then shutting it down because— Buttigieg: You couldn’t get your way. I mean, even that, right? He becomes chairman of the premier cultural institution in the country, and within two years, it’s shutting down, right? But my point is, there’s something broader, bigger, and longer in the making than even Trump and Trumpism itself. Like, if I said the system is broken, that’ll be the least memorable thing I said all day, right? “The system is broken” is a cliché. Everybody says it. Certainly, every politician says it because it’s true. The weird thing is, we say the system is broken, and then we act as if it wasn’t. We go right back to normal and— Goldberg: The system is broken. What’s for lunch? Buttigieg: Yeah, right? Like, if you’re getting ready for a flight and the captain comes on and mentions in passing that the airplane is broken— You’re not going to stay on the plane, right? Goldberg: So that’s one of the things you worked on as secretary of transportation, right? Was that a bigger problem than we know about? Because that would be really useful. Buttigieg: No, but that’s the point, right? When lots of planes were crashing in the ’60s and ’70s and ’80s, we did something about it and developed this incredible, civilizational achievement of aviation safety, because we didn’t think it was acceptable to have that happen. And here we are in this political system where everybody knows that the role of money in politics, the unrepresentative nature of Congress, what the Supreme Court has turned into, is a huge problem. And eight years ago, when I was talking about things like Supreme Court reform—pretty lonely position—everybody was looking at me funny. Part of why I was doing that was not only because I believe in Supreme Court reform, but because I believe in elevating our ambition as a country when it comes to reforming things that are obviously broken. And look, at a political level, most of the time, most of what people like me should be talking about is the fact that diesel is at the highest price ever recorded, and the fact that interest rates are higher because of the policies of this administration, and the fact that most of us are paying higher health-insurance premiums because of what the Republicans are doing. But the reality is, those kitchen-table issues have become intractable, because you’ve got this political system kicking the legs out from under the kitchen table. And when people see that day after day after day, then when they see some cases of spectacular corruption by the first family, it’s just all part of that wash of low expectations that we’ve come to have for our politics and our system. Goldberg: Is the system broken, or are people broken? Buttigieg: The system is more broken than people. Goldberg: But when I say people, I mean specific people, like the members of Congress. Buttigieg: Okay, well, some of them. But here’s the thing. Goldberg: I don’t mean our audience. They’re not broken. They look great. Buttigieg: It would be an extraordinary run of bad luck if all of the people that we have elected to Congress over the past few years just randomly turned out to be terrible people, right? That would be just statistically— Goldberg: You say that like that’s science fiction, but I don’t know, I mean, but it doesn’t sound that incredible to me either. Buttigieg: Either something about the system makes people worse, or something about the system is actively selecting worse people, right? It’s not just bad luck, right? And I say this having dealt with the United States Congress quite a bit, and you can see how even people who start out good are incentivized to become less so over time. You sit in a committee hearing, and it’s basically a content farm now. You go in hoping to have some legislative progress, and they look at you and tell you you’re right, and then explain why it’s never going to happen. So we can keep wondering why the people who our system produces and pushes into leadership are deeply flawed, or we can do something to create a system where it’s better than that. Goldberg: I’m focusing on Congress because Congress has abdicated its oversight responsibility to a large degree. The judiciary is—put aside the Supreme Court for a second; you do have federal judges checking executive power. But Congress, in its current form, ain’t. So what do you do? Buttigieg: Let me explain what I mean. Yeah, obviously, in the near term, what I mean is elect better people to Congress, which is why I’ve been to most U.S. states at least once in the past year and a half, trying to campaign for people who I think would make better representatives in Congress than the ones that we’ve got. So there’s that. But what I really mean is: Design a better Congress. For example, one way to make sure that you get better and not worse people in a job is to have a very competitive hiring process. We have 435 seats in the House. Roughly 40 of them are competitive, which means nine out of 10 congressional races are races where you pretty much know who’s going to win before the first vote is even cast. In a 50/50 country, right? Which means it is not an authentic, competitive process. Goldberg: It is the most cost-effective job-protection system ever devised. Buttigieg: Yeah. So we ought to ban gerrymandering nationally. We ought to ban gerrymandering. And the last reason to have any hesitation about doing that fell when they gutted the Voting Rights Act in the Callais decision, because there was, up until then, an argument that, Look, because these states are badly gerrymandered anyway, the only way we’re getting minority representation is to protect minority districts. Now the Supreme Court has basically established a de facto right to partisan gerrymandering. We can have a law that changes that, right? The role of money in politics, obviously part of the problem here. These are not things that are completely beyond our power to deal with. Goldberg: Well, the Supreme Court has basically said it’s beyond your power, on the role of money in politics. Buttigieg: Right. So we should have a constitutional amendment on that. Buttigieg: We had a constitutional amendment so that you couldn’t have a drink. And then, like, 10 years later, we thought that was dumb. And we changed it with another constitutional amendment. So this idea that it is somehow beyond our power to use what is probably the single most elegant feature of the Constitution, which is its capacity to be amended, is a complete failure of ambition and imagination. There are some other things we can do in the meantime. I was in Montana helping gather signatures for an effort called the Montana Plan, which is using, because of what the Supreme Court has said, instead of setting it up as a regulation on speech—since the Supreme Court has said that, you know, money is speech and corporations are people—you go to the fact that corporations only exist as a function of state law, and corporations actually in state law don’t have rights; they have powers, and those powers are enumerated. And so in the law that makes corporations exist, you just have a feature in there that basically says, You don’t get to be a corporation if you are using your Treasury dollars to manipulate elections. That’s one example of something that is designed to have an effect while we don’t yet have a constitutional amendment. Think of it this way: Citizens United came down, I believe, in 2010. So if the counter to a constitutional-amendment process is That could take 15 years, well, if we’d started in 2010, we’d be there, right? This is a level of ambition I think we need to have, or else every time we’re trying to make sure that more people can get health insurance or the minimum wage, which hasn’t been raised, I think, since 2009 or 2007, actually goes up—or any other outcome which a majority of Americans already agree—we’re going to keep getting frustrated. Goldberg: I want to ask you something about an even deeper structure. You know, James Madison had a lot of fears, which is why he designed the system we have. James Madison feared that the rise of the daily newspaper would sink democracy, because the once-a-day addition of new information into the human brain was too much for people. You couldn’t go from emotion to reason before new information came the next day. We’re beyond that now. We’re well beyond that. And so the question is—it’s a cognitive question. One of the tricks of the Trump administration is to do so much so quickly that you reach overload. The other part of this is, in the social-media environment in which we have, is it irrational or a sign of poor character for a member of Congress to say, You know what, I’m not going out on a limb, because I’m going to get 10,000 death threats a day on social media, and I exist in that content farm, in another form of that content farm? So what do you do to reintroduce the possibility, the space for reason, logic, contemplation? Buttigieg: I’ve come to realize that we have different levels within us of thinking, of consciousness. And part of the problem with social media is that it speaks to the lizard brain, right? Kind of the least contemplative of our levels of thinking. And there’s no question that the system of rewards and punishments that is kind of dominant in the digital world is not healthy for politics. But that is not the only realm where we can practice politics. So one thing that we can do more of is engage in actual spaces—real, physical spaces where people are actually in a room. And politics is often at its best when the public square is physical. I think about it in terms of how I travel, just when I’m walking around town or going through an airport. And 99.9 percent of people who come up to me are very positive. And that includes some people who say, I don’t agree with you politically, but it’s nice to see you. They’re just kind of excited to see somebody they recognize. Buttigieg: Yeah. And it’s not because obviously I have 99 percent support. I know this is a divided country. What that tells me is most people in an actual 3-D interaction aren’t assholes, right? Very different from how it is online. So I think one very simple thing is to try to cultivate and create more spaces and environments where those interactions happen. That’s in-person convenings, that’s the public square, that’s third spaces, that’s building social capital around existing physical spaces that bring people together, like schools. It’s also something that is part of why I believe in ideas like national service, creating more opportunities for service. Because when I was in the military, that was definitely an environment where I was side by side with people that I had wildly different political values from, and we quickly learned to trust each other with our lives. And part of that had to do with the extreme pressure of being in a war zone. But part of that just had to do with the fact that this group of people was thrown together and tasked with doing something hard. You know, not everybody needs to be in the military for that to be available to more people. It’s why I think a voluntary but ultimately universal opportunity for service would do a lot of good. It would just rewire our brains in a healthier fashion. Goldberg: The answer to Is Donald Trump a symptom or a cause? is both. Yeah, obviously. The question is what percentage is symptom and what percentage is cause? Is it possible to disaggregate that? Buttigieg: I think of it in terms of the broad symptoms and then the cause of some acute problems. So the symptom stuff—this is what I’m getting at with my systems and reform talk: that if the economic and political and social systems in this country were working, we wouldn’t be here. You don’t get a guy like Trump just out of nowhere, right? So, that’s the evidence. Goldberg: Let’s pause on that. What system in particular, or what aspect of the system would have—I don’t mean the cultural system, rise of reality TV and social media—but what part of the system would have stopped this? Buttigieg: The part I think is worst is the part where in a supposedly representative system of government, people reach consensus, or at least overwhelming majority, that certain things should happen, and then they don’t. That is lethal to people’s confidence in their political system, and it leads, sooner or later, to a pattern where they say, You know what? I’m just going to vote to burn the house down because none of this is working. By the way, just a quick political digression here—it is really interesting that most of those big kind of two-to-one things are actually things that are Democratic positions in Washington. So if I think about, like I mentioned earlier, raising the minimum wage—raising the minimum wage at least to $12 is something a majority of Republicans believe we should do. Background checks on guns: 70, 80, 90 percent. Eighty percent of Republicans, gun owners, think we ought to at least do that. Tax fairness, where the wealthiest people are paying at least as much in their taxes as a percentage as a firefighter—overwhelming majority. And what I’ve realized is we’re actually—Democrats are still getting used to being on the right side of the American public on a lot of issues. Because when I came of age, right—like now, everybody’s pretty much with us on taxes and health care—you know, when I came of age, we were on defense about things like the death tax and welfare reform and all of that. And my first time ever on the ballot was 2010, when Obamacare was the toxic issue that killed the Democrats, right? Now it is the winning issue for Democrats. So some of this is new, and I think Democrats aren’t used to that. But my bigger point is you have all these things where there’s actually two-to-one agreement among the American people on what to do. By the way, even immigration—this is true—the broad framework of a grand bargain on immigration that’s been there the whole time, and then nothing like that happens in Washington. That breeds a kind of cynicism that I think leads you to a guy like Trump. But then back to the cause part. Even a guy like that didn’t have to do things like this. Like, you could imagine some disagreeable populist leader who is presented with the possibility that AI may threaten the continued existence of the human species and does not decide to call that a hoax. It really matters that we happen to have a president who does. Or if you think about what’s happening, if you’re imagining the calculations that are going on in Beijing right now, or Moscow, obviously they are preparing to take advantage of some specific issues that are attached to having a specifically unstable person running the United States of America. And that’s not just some, you know, world historical force. That’s the particular misfortune we have of being led by this particular man at this particular moment. Goldberg: Right. They’re clapping for instability. I want to come back to the global picture in a second, but stay on this interesting subject of the Democrats. There’s been a switch, you’re right. Mainstream Democratic positions are, generally speaking, the more popular positions. How are the Democrats going to mess up in the next two months so that they don’t take Congress back? Buttigieg: Look, I feel really good about the tailwind that we have in the next two months. I was just in Tucson, Arizona, where I think JoAnna Mendoza, who’s a Marine drill instructor and a mom, is going to defeat the Republican congressman there. Then we were in southern New Mexico, where we have Gabe Vasquez, extraordinary frontline Democrat. I think he’s going to earn reelection. Just last night, I was up in Tarrytown with Cait Conley, West Pointer, [who] I think is going to defeat Mike Lawler. We have great candidates running great campaigns. What could go wrong? Two things. Well, lots of things could go wrong between now and the election. I’m mostly trying to make sure—obviously I spend all day, pretty much, when I’m campaigning, trying to make sure things go right at the election. What about after the election? Two things I think would be a terrible mistake. One is to revert to the status quo, the previous status quo. I really think that’s going to be a strong temptation to say, Okay, we’re in; we’re going to reverse the damage. You hear the phrase reverse the damage, right? Which is understandable because the damage has been horrific. But the implication of reverse the damage, if that’s going to be our mantra, is, Okay, we’re going to put all these things back just the way they were, and we’re going to give you the world the way it looked in 2022 or 2012 or 1994, or whenever we think America was great. And that’s not going to work. So we’ve got to make sure we resist the temptation to be restoring some previous status quo and build for the new reality that we’re in, with the next quarter century between here in 2050, which is kind of a make-or-break quarter century for the whole American project, is subject to very different conditions and problems than the ones that we inherited. The other thing that I think we need to do is keep it simple in terms of a legislative agenda. So I’ve been thinking a lot about these. For example, one thing that the chattering class will get into right away as soon as there’s hopefully a Democratic victory, is what’s going to be H.R. 1? What’s going to be the big, telltale, Christmas-tree, signature bill that you put all your hopes and dreams in that signifies the priorities of the party and of the new Congress, right? To me, I would like to see, like an H.R. 1, 2, 3, 4, 5—like 10 or 12 dozen things. Stand-alone bills, clean bills, not this ponderous 2,000-page thing that tries to take care of every single thing, but a set of things that are needed, popular, and wise, and will make a difference to people, especially in being able to afford everyday life. So, what does that look like? Restoring the child-tax-credit extension. That puts money back in the pockets of lower-income families trying to raise kids. Obviously, getting us out of a war in Iran that has everybody paying more for gas and diesel. Restoring the help on health care. By the way, also, on the tax side—so usually the revenue part’s unpopular, so you have to tuck that in with the spending part that’s popular. Right now, fixing the tax code so that wealthy tax cheats couldn’t get away with it or even truing up the rates to be a little more normal for wealthy individuals or for corporations—those things are popular on their own. Even if you didn’t staple it to how you’re going to spend the money, just the tax side alone is popular because it’s the right thing to do, and because everybody can tell there’s something wrong with the way things are going. So to me, a rapid fire of clean bills to do these things that everybody—raise the minimum wage, right—these things that everybody knows we ought to do, that’s how we move forward. How do we go wrong? We get tangled up, get in our own way, make it super complicated, and people don’t feel like we’re doing what we actually, hopefully will have gotten elected on, which is making everyday life better, and in particular, making everyday life more affordable. Buttigieg: I mean, look, I think there’s a temptation to imagine that being bold is the same thing as being kind of maximally ideological. To me, sometimes that might be true; sometimes the boldest idea maybe is the leftmost idea. Sometimes I think we’ve reached a point where actually, what I would consider to be kind of ideologically moderate projects—like everything I mentioned, is kind of moderate by definition because two-thirds of Americans want to do it—requires some radical reform to our institutions. And so there are big swings we need to be taking with some of the ones I talked about on reform in the Supreme Court and money and politics and gerrymandering, but also big policy swings, right? I mean, eight years ago, the whole Democratic primary fight in the debates that I was in was about was universal health care going to become mandatory or was universal health care just going to be optional? And not much has changed since then, right? So we also just need to have a higher level of ambition. To me that’s the most interesting thing. I know the commentariat wants to get it all fixed on some left-to-center-to-right spectrum. To me, what’s more interesting is do we have the right package of base hits and big swings, and are we going after both of them at the same time all the time? Goldberg: In the few minutes we have left, talk about the fact that we’re already in World War III and don’t even know it, and while we’re in World War III, the president of the United States is making enemies out of our allies. Goldberg: Yeah, you have four minutes—I mean, because this is the Alice in Wonderland cut. We’re trying to have a rational conversation about all the things that you could do to make the lives of Americans somewhat better, you know? And, we have a president right now who seems to want to go to war against Canada, which is a South Park episode. I mean, the question is like, we’re having this conversation, but we’re living in a reality that is South Park. Buttigieg: First of all, every time we see one of these things, we have to remember it doesn’t have to be this way. It doesn’t have to be this way. And politically, what I’m trying to do is connect these things that are happening around the world to issues at home. Because foreign policy always codes as this kind of high-minded, kind of elite concern. But the reality is, foreign policy and what’s going on in the world hits us at home in a million different ways. Like my neighbors in Michigan, you know, a year ago, nobody filling up the pump necessarily had to know or care what was going on in the Strait of Hormuz. It’s not your problem. Now you’ve got gas closer to $5 than $4 in Michigan. Diesel’s through the roof. This is a direct relationship right between what’s going on in the world and what’s going on at home. I was talking with union members in the hospitality industry in Las Vegas, cocktail servers getting fewer hours. Las Vegas has one of the highest unemployment rates in Nevada; generally, in the U.S. Why? Well, one of the reasons is, pissing off Canada meant that Canadians who normally come to southern Nevada, especially in the wintertime, decided to take their money elsewhere, right? So there are these very direct effects on working Americans that happen when you have an absurd foreign policy, like we do. And the result is that it leaves us worse off in the near term economically at home, as well as, of course, the bigger, longer-term picture, which is, we don’t know exactly how and when we’ll need our allies next, but we know that we’ll need our allies. And having alienated them, it’s realistically going to take a generation to have the right level of credibility. Goldberg: This is the question. I mean, let’s assume for a minute that, you know, there’s a Democratic president in the beginning of 2029. How much of what’s happened overseas is reversible and how much is just—I guess the biggest question is, are we, right now, undergoing a midlife crisis, a nervous breakdown, or are we facing a terminal illness? And if you’re sitting in Canada, Paris, London, South Korea, Japan, you’re asking yourself, I don’t know anymore about these people. Buttigieg: Yeah, no, that’s definitely what’s happening. I’ve talked to leaders who—one, who just looked at me and said, “Look, the first time you guys did this, it was,”—this is of a U.S. ally—“it was a blip. I thought of it as a blip. Now it’s a pattern and our country has to prepare.” And obviously, this scenario where you have a Democratic president next, everybody’s going to be saying, Okay, that’s great, you’re saying all the right things now. But, you know. what’s going to happen in four years? Which is exactly why reversible is not the word on my mind. I’m trying to purge my vocabulary of just about anything with the prefix re in it. But, again, this doesn’t have to be 100 percent negative, because we’re entering into a world where the old patterns and institutions are not coming back. And a lot of those institutions were pretty rickety to begin with. The UN Security Council was set up at a time when the vast majority of people in China were impoverished rural peasants, right? Now, a middle-class city dweller in China arguably has a more technologically sophisticated everyday life than a middle-class city dweller in America. We’re in a very different world in terms of technology, in terms of competition, in terms of the security balance. And so we’re going to need to revamp—there I go, re again. We’re going to need to—I think reimagine is okay. Goldberg: What about refried beans? How do you feel? Goldberg: Just going out for fried beans from now on. Buttigieg: But I think there’s an opportunity, so it comes back to what I was saying earlier. You know, interpersonally, my experience is you can build trust in a hurry if you are working together on something hard and important. That’s true for people. That’s true for countries. So in addition to the generational project of earning trust and credibility by just consistently doing the right thing and being a dependable friend and ally and a sober voice in the world, there’s also no shortage of very difficult things that we need to be, starting with the response to AI—no shortage of very difficult things where we need to be working with friends, allies, partners, and, in some cases, the whole world. And I think we can quickly earn trust, in the same way that the entire pattern of the U.S. being the most respected and indispensable partner—that was kind of the pattern of the second half of the 20th century—really was built up in a matter of just a few years of doing the right thing when it was really hard. I actually see in that an opportunity, precisely because of how bad it is. I see an opportunity to build something that is going to serve us well for the balance of this century. Pete, we can do this all day, but we can’t, unfortunately. So, thank you very much for coming.

Every time humanity invents a tool that fundamentally changes how we live, a wave of panic follows. When writing was invented Socrates warned it would ruin our memories. When the printing press arrived, critics claimed that an overload of books would make people foolish and immoral. steam trains were feared, with experts claiming that traveling at the high speed of 30 miles per hour would cause the human body to disintegrate or drive passengers insane. In every case, these technologies did not destroy us; they simply became normal parts of daily life. Todays fears about AI are being heavily pushed by the tech companies themselves, but for a very different reason. By keeping everyone focused on sci-fi scenarios about killer robots ending the world, these corporations divert attention from immediate & real-world lawsuits. They are actively lobbying governments for protection against copyright theft claims and suing for immunity when their systems cause real world harm. Calling for strict safety regulations allows them to create a "regulatory moat", making it too expensive and legally risky for smaller competitors or open-source projects to survive, while keeping the big players legally protected.I don't entirely agree with you, but you make some good points and it's an interesting take. More on topic, I think cloud customers should be aware that if their datacenter is destroyed, they *will* lose data if they didn't take care of replicating their data to a different geolocation. Exactly the same happened when an OVH datacenter caught on fire in France. Many customers which didn't have replication set to a different geolocation lost their data. Heck a dude I know had backups on different servers in the same datacenter which turned out to be useless. I replicate my filesystem to different geolo While at it, take snapshots on the remote server to prevent against ransomware attacks and your prod server shouldn't have any access to the replication server in a different geolocation otherwise hackers will delete/encrypt data on the replication server too. The replication server should pull the data from the prod server with something like this: CMD_OUTPUT=`ssh -C -l root -p 52812 $HOST zfs send rpool/data/${ZVOL}@${NEW_SNAP_NAME} | zfs recv rpool/datarep/${ZVOL} 2>&1` I know it's possible to give the prod server access to the replication server like in the proxmox backup server architecture and set permissions on the replication server so snapshots can't be deleted but I find it much more safer to protect the replication server in its own VLAN and protect it almost like a CA and insure nobody and nothing has access to the replication server. This is why I still like the (very) old fashion tape backupp systems. Just backup to tape every day (And preferably a copy kept in a bank safe or whatever. In case your office literally burns down or whatever) and if you get ransomware, just send "Fuck you" to the attacker, go to the safe, restore, be done with it. Granted the cloud... not so easy.
