They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.
2026-09-18

They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The facade of the building betrays no affiliation—neither hammer nor sickle. At 235 W. 23rd St. in Manhattan, the lobby is similarly spartan. All the signage is in black and white, the walls various shades of gray. A private equity firm resides on the fifth floor; an A.I. firm takes up the penthouse property on eight. The third and fourth floors have tenants as well. The directory says that on the seventh floor, there is the building’s management company, an education company, two publishers, and something else: the “Offices of the CPUSA”—that is, the headquarters of the Communist Party USA. On the occasion of America’s 250th anniversary, President Donald Trump took time away from the birthday revelry to warn of a grave and imposing adversary. “Communism is a mortal threat to American liberty. It is the greatest threat to our country, including World War I, World War II, Pearl Harbor, or even 9/11,” he said on July Fourth weekend this year, in South Dakota. That’s an awfully big threat, you might think, but the president is way ahead of you. “I think it’s the greatest threat that I’ve ever seen to our country,” he told a Georgia audience a few days before. In fact, according to the Atlantic, Trump mentioned communism or communist 15 times on that Georgia soapbox, “starting less than a minute into his hour-plus speech.” All summer long he’s been worked up, not over the antifa anarchists or the DSA socialists but over the commies in particular. He’s not alone. Secretary of State Marco Rubio is also riled by the Red hunt—he’s been convening politicians and law-enforcement officers and counterterrorism experts from across the globe to combat the “transnational threat.” And recently confirmed Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin has seen the communists on our very own liberty-loving shores. He went on Fox News with a big-time scoop: He had identified the Communist Party logo on a “No Kings” rally flyer. “No Kings rally is literally sponsored by the Communist Party,” he ascertained. Red Scare politics are back, about that there is no question. But where, exactly, are those scary Reds who have infiltrated and organized and outflanked the administration? Certainly, it seems, they are everywhere. But individually, they are extremely difficult to find. They are not doing high-profile sabotage of corporations; they are not shooting it out in the coal mines. One place they definitely are, though, is in this building in Manhattan, right out there in the open for anyone to see. And so, on a late morning in late August, I buzzed the seventh floor and rode the elevator straight into the belly of the beast. In the middle of the CPUSA office, sitting at a table in a conference room with four walls of glass, was Joe Sims, the party’s co-chair, wearing a black newsboy cap. In front of him stood a walker. Across the table was another comrade, youthful office assistant Brooklyn Crawford. Usually, it would be slightly more bustling than this, Sims assured me. But, as it was late August, these were the only two people in the office, save for the odd delivery guy, dropping off a package. Already, this was a deeper trip into the inner sanctum than anyone in the Trump administration had managed. CPUSA co-chair Sims told me that despite the president’s pugilism, there had not yet been any Justice Department demands, nor congressional subpoenas, nor even right-wing agitators out front. “It’s not a crime to be a Communist in this country,” he said. Around the office were vestiges of times gone by. The walls were decorated with a framed cartoon of Vladimir Lenin* sweeping kings and capitalist fat cats from the Earth, and a charcoal drawing of a man with a mallet pointing to a bull’s-eye reading “$60,000,” part of a onetime fundraising drive. There was a stylized poster of Che Guevara celebrating a little-known holiday on Oct. 8—Day of the Heroic Guerrilla. Elsewhere, Soviet realist–style paintings hung. Gifts from Cuba, a plate from the Communist Party of Vietnam (given “with compliments”), and various statuettes and busts adorned the countertops and bookshelves. Framed photos of bygone leadership. There was a small kitchen, with a sign taped to the fridge: “Please clean up after yourself no maid service here!!!” Out of the back windows was a view of the Edge NYC, the flagship high-rise property of Hudson Yards, with its protuberant observation deck. Out of the front windows was the distinctive facade of the Chelsea Hotel. The office, Crawford told me, had been designed for transparency and decked out with sustainable materials: bamboo, linoleum tables made from linseed oil, recycled carpeting, and the glass-walled conference room, all in service of an environmentally aligned approach. I settled into a chair between comrade Sims and comrade Crawford and asked them about the party’s political program, the path toward revolution—a winding path, no doubt, but one that has required incredible endurance. The party has survived the Red Scare of the 1950s, the Cold War, and the Soviet collapse, and is somehow over 100 years old. And now it is readying another Red Scare. Let’s get this out of the way quickly: The USA Communist Party, contra Trump and Co., is not a major player in American politics these days, behind the scenes or out front. It works with local tenants’ groups, it boosts candidates, it piggybacks on the Democratic Socialists of America sometimes, but sometimes not. Its numbers have grown over the past decade, but not overwhelmingly. But arguably more important than the art collection or the political strategy, and perhaps more important even than the dues collection, in explaining the Communist Party’s unlikely longevity, is the building we were in itself. Because the CPUSA is not merely a tenant holding down an office on a surprisingly posh block of Manhattan’s very gilt-edged neighborhood of Chelsea. The CPUSA is the owner of the office. In fact, the Communist Party is owner of the entire building at 235 W. 23rd St., a flagship commercial property now valued at nearly $10 million. And one of the party’s essential functions at the moment, as it has been for the past 20 years, is something Trump could well understand: It is a commercial landlord, collecting rents from commercial tenants on the building’s other floors. The Communists and the president, those bitter adversaries, might have more in common than they think. But unlike Trump, the Communists really, really hate talking about it. The story of the CPUSA cannot be told without getting into the quirks and particularities of Gus Hall. In 1959 Hall was first elected secretary general of the party, a title he held until his death in 2000. Hall ran for president (of the United States) four times: twice in the 1970s, twice in the 1980s. His best finish came in 1976, when he netted just shy of 59,000 votes, though the exact count is contested. He was the genuine artifact, the son of an impoverished Minnesota miner, and he served as a lumberjack and a steelworker in his youth. Hall led the party through the exceedingly challenging times of the Cold War era, the aftermath of McCarthyism, and the subsequent collapse of the Soviet Union. In some sense, it is a miracle that the party survived, though it has held on in a deeply diminished fashion. Although Hall long maintained that the CPUSA was a fully independent entity, a Moscow newspaper reported in 1992 that Hall had been the recipient of $40 million in Soviet assistance between 1971 and 1990. “The struggle between those who own the wealth and those whose labor produces the wealth is one flaw in capitalism that will lead to socialism,” Hall said in 1996. And Hall took his own sermonizing to heart. In the late 1970s, during that period of Soviet patronage, Hall decided to get into real estate. He purchased the building on 23rd Street, according to public records, in the 1970s, taking out a $150,000 mortgage on the property at an 8 percent interest rate. That wasn’t his only shrewd money move. There were other properties Hall and the party owned too. Sims, meanwhile, told me that Hall also got the party into the stock market. Even in the 1970s, Hall had a reputation for “playing around with stocks,” Sims said. Under the leadership of Hall and national Chairman Henry Winston, the party raised capital funds from comrades, which were then invested. So while his politicking may have left something to be desired—the party bled membership while he celebrated North Korea and various Stalinist ideas—his asset accumulation proved visionary. “We have investments,” Sims told me, declining to further elaborate on the portfolio. For a long time, the CPUSA occupied the entire Chelsea building, despite a dwindling headcount. And when the 1907-built edifice required maintenance, some party members did not view it as a blessing. In a September 2001 report to the board, a year after Hall’s death, the party took stock of its holding. “Our building is a wonderful asset, a real treasure for our Party and the working class movement of which we are all immensely proud. It’s situated in a prime location, midtown Manhattan, worth million[s] in today’s market.” But having five-digit annual operating expenses “puts a tremendous burden on Party finances,” the report found. “You often hear that the building has a shabby appearance. Unfortunately we would have to admit this is all too true. The reality is we can’t keep on top of it. The building has been under capitalized for years.” Finally, the report resolves: Though the party “still [has] to be alert to the enemy,” the “decline in anti-communism has created new opportunities to utilize our building in a new way.” In other words: to lease it out. And so the revolutionaries became rent-seekers. Hall once famously said he was forced to buy his home in Yonkers—under his wife’s name, in 1960, for $21,000—because no landlord “would rent to a Communist leader.” Now the Communists were on the other side. By 2007, they were in the process of withdrawing from eight to four floors, after having spent $1 million on renovations, and renting the other four out. By 2026, they had vacated seven of eight. On the seventh floor, the new Red Square had not quite arrived. But it is worth being clear that this is not simply White House bluster or Fox News filler. Republicans in Congress, on the Ways and Means Committee, have subpoenaed various left-wing groups as part of an investigation into foreign funding linked to the Chinese Communist Party. But that dogged search for connections to the Chinese Communist Party has not led Republicans to the American Communist Party. “So far, thankfully, no,” Sims said. “As long as we pay our taxes, pay our electrical bill, I think we’re OK. “We have what we call fraternal ties with other Communist Parties, but we don’t have any economic relationships. Nor do these other groups, by the way.” When Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders sparked the left-wing reawakening, in 2016, the CPUSA membership was nearly even with that of the fledgling Democratic Socialists of America, both sleepy holdovers of a bygone political moment. According to Gus Hall’s obituary in the New York Times in 2000, Communist Party enrollment “had dwindled to about 3,000, down from a peak of about 66,000 in 1939.” In 1962 a full 17 percent of the membership were reported to be federal informants. “Since 2016, we’ve recruited 20,000 people that have applied for membership in the party,” Sims told me, though the party has long suffered from overstatement when it comes to self-reported membership numbers. “I think about 4,000 people have joined since 2022,” he added, a more modest addendum. According to the party’s website, about a third of those pay dues. But even if that’s a generous figure, it’s paltry compared to the growth of the DSA over that same time period. In a decade, the group has gone from roughly 6,000 members to 120,000, marking some of its biggest gains in the past year or so. “I’m jealous,” Sims said. Critically, the DSA doesn’t just have members; it also has elected officials. At last count, there were six DSA members in the New York state Assembly, three DSA state senators, and four DSAers on the New York City Council. Bigger, perhaps, than all is the New York City mayor’s mansion, where democratic socialist Zohran Mamdani resides. And that’s just in New York: Soon, there will likely be DSA-affiliated members of Congress from Colorado, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and more. That is high on the to-do list for the CPUSA. “We got away from running candidates in the 1980s,” Sims told me. “That’s something we’d like to get back to.” “If we were to win an election, in a major city, or even in a midsize city … it would’ve been on the front page of the New York Times. It’s one thing for a Socialist to win, but for a Communist, oh my God,” he said. Running candidates is not the only area in which the group differs with the DSA, despite the shared affinity for the color red. On some issues there is agreement, and the two groups work in coalition. “Not an ideological configuration but a practical one,” said Sims. DSA groups sometimes come and use the office when there is strategic alignment and a need for a venue. But not always. “On some things we don’t agree, like on housing. We’re for public housing. They’re for a scheme which goes in the direction of privatization,” said Sims, referring to RAD/PACT, a policy supported by the mayor that would see Section 9, or traditional public housing, converted to Section 8, which the city owns but doesn’t directly fund or manage. Another point of difference would be the property itself. While property ownership has been a controversy-causer in the DSA of late—the New York Post has sought, perhaps effectively, to gin up outrage over DSA co-Chair Gustavo Gordillo’s ownership of a Brooklyn home purchased by his parents—the CPUSA is not losing sleep over implications of hypocrisy with regard to its own property ownership. “People outside of leftist spaces will be like, ‘How do you call yourself a Communist—you have an iPhone?’ We all live in a society where we’re trying to live and survive and participate,” said Crawford. For now, the party sees the Republican Party in a creep toward fascism, and forestalling that is a top priority. As such, the Communists remain interested in helping Democrats win elections. That means voter turnout, as well as coalition-building on certain issues. “We’re not so much interested in a Democratic victory for its own sake, but we’re for pushing back that danger as much as possible. We live in the real world, and in the real world there are differences between the two parties on issues that affect working people,” Sims said. A Democratic majority in the House would, among other things, probably cool off the Red Scare redux. “The Democratic Party, though it has a lot of weaknesses, they haven’t joined the choir,” he said. Flipping the House to a Democratic majority would get the gavel away from Republican Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith and put an end to his Joe McCarthy impression. This office, though quiet on this Thursday, does get used—it is not just “a museum of history,” as Gus Hall used to call it. The Young Communist League meets there; some tenant groups from local public housing units will gather there as well. The party continues to publish People’s World, the publication once (and perhaps better) known as the Daily Worker. Is the publication funded primarily by rents raised from the office building? I asked. “You keep coming back to the building,” Sims said. “No.” It’s true—I kept coming back to the building. I couldn’t help it. A website mocked up by commercial real estate brokerage RDE Advisors celebrates its many advantages. “12’ ceilings throughout, with a spectacular 16’ ceiling penthouse on the 8th floor,” it reads. “What could 235 W. 23rd become?” asks another part of the site. “AI Tech Headquarters” and “Fashion Agency” are among the suggestions. The building boasts 55,362 square feet of rentable space, roughly 6,766 per floor. The site claims that all of the floors are vacant/available or soon will be, including the Communist Party’s seventh, which is not correct. The asking price—$39 per square foot, per year—looked like a bargain when compared cursorily to other available office space in the neighborhood listed online. “This is one of the few remaining opportunities in Manhattan to consolidate your entire organization under one roof,” the site says. “The landlord will consider leasing floors individually.” Crawford, the assistant, clarified to me that they would rent to most people, “though we wouldn’t rent to Turning Point USA.” The leasing agents didn’t respond to my requests for comment. I reached out to all of the tenants as well, to ask what it was like to have the Communist Party as a landlord. Only Ross Cameron, co-founder of Lyric Capital Group, a private equity firm focused on music royalties, housed on four, responded. “This email made me laugh out loud! Didn’t know there was a connection with the owner to the Communist Party and honestly didn’t know there was still an American Communist Party!” he said. What Did the Kennedy Center Ever Do to Donald Trump?
2026-09-17

What Did the Kennedy Center Ever Do to Donald Trump?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Is it curtains for the Kennedy Center? Since retaking office, Donald Trump has demanded that the capital city’s chief performing arts venue be remade in his image—and threatened to shutter it if he doesn’t get his way. This week the fight came to a head: A federal judge rejected Trump’s latest efforts to rename the building after himself and demanded advance notice if he tries to knock it down. Whether Trump complies will not only determine the Kennedy Center’s fate. It’s also shaping up to be an unlikely test of whether the rule of law can constrain a rogue president. It’s the nation’s official cultural center. Created by an act of Congress and named in honor of President John F. Kennedy, the center’s white-marbled building has hosted plays, concerts, and award ceremonies since 1971. He has moved quickly to put his stamp on the center. Citing “woke” programming, he replaced its bipartisan board of trustees with sycophants last year. The new board named Trump as chairman, and he helped plan performances and awardees. An acclaimed children’s musical about a shark who “wants to let out his inner fish” was out; Sylvester Stallone was in. Dozens of prominent artists nixed performances or severed ties with the center and criticized Trump’s attempts to align it with his politics. Things turned litigious after Trump’s handpicked board voted last year to rename the center after him, adding his name to the facade and changing its online logo to “The Trump-Kennedy Center.” Not well. In May, a federal judge said that only Congress could rename the center and temporarily blocked Trump’s plan to close it for a $257 million renovation. An appeals court refused to overrule the judge. So the board came back with a new idea: appending “Restored and Renovated by President Donald J. Trump” to the center’s name and renaming its grounds for him. And the president himself issued an ultimatum: If his name doesn’t go on the building, he won’t help save it. Like many performance venues, the center’s finances suffered during the pandemic, and the administration has pointed to the threat of imminent bankruptcy to justify Trump’s takeover. But the takeover accelerated the center’s downward spiral in ticket sales and fundraising. The administration also claims that “severe structural deterioration” could require tearing down the building, citing an incident in which a chunk of ceiling collapsed during a recent storm. “I think that the Trump administration should certainly have recognition,” the president told reporters yesterday. “Because frankly, if we don’t do that, it’s going to close. It’ll end up being ripped down.” That’s a pretty naked threat. Would he really do that? If he doesn’t get his way, Trump seems more than willing to ruin the Kennedy Center for everybody else. After the same federal judge rejected the board’s latest naming workaround on Tuesday, the administration abruptly shuttered the building. (It claims the closure is temporary.) Yesterday, Trump was photographed reviewing a poster that appeared to show a plan to demolish it. On Thursday, the judge demanded 30 days’ notice before the administration makes any major structural changes to the center, including tearing it down. But Trump has taken advantage of the slow-grinding gears of our legal system before. Last year he demolished the East Wing of the White House to make way for a gargantuan ballroom before courts could stop him. Whether he takes a wrecking ball to it or lets it stagnate, the Kennedy Center threatens to become his latest victim. If that happens, it’ll not only gut a cultural landmark; it’ll be another example of the president losing in court but getting his way anyway. That’s a troubling sign for the rule of law. Last question: There’s a war on, gas is $4.43 a gallon, and the midterms are around the corner. Why is Trump so obsessed with the Kennedy Center? The fight over a performing arts venue might not matter electorally, but it’s of a piece with Trump’s personality and priorities. The president is a pop-culture fanatic; he blasts Broadway showtunes at his rallies and was tweetstorming about Kristen Stewart a few years before becoming the leader of the free world. At times, Trump has seemed more focused on his ballroom than on the war he started with Iran. It’s an object lesson that fits a broader pattern: For more than a decade now, one man’s flights of fancy have inevitably become the country’s pressing concerns. The Kennedy Center might not (yet) bear Trump’s name. But this is his world, and we’re just living in it. The potential loss of yet another national landmark got you down? For your evening enjoyment, my colleagues have some ideas about what to focus on instead. Dive into a true-crime longread: As a teenager, Winston Walters became a rising reality-TV star for wrestling giant catfish. But shortly after his 20th birthday, he was arrested for a grisly attack—which he strongly denies he committed—and sentenced to 92 years in prison. His case became a cause célèbre among fans and online content creators. Crime reporter Wes Ferguson spent months speaking with Walters, his supporters, and the crime’s victims to make sense of the case’s twists and turns. It’s a gripping read. Crack open a new book: Emily St. John Mandel, author of Station Eleven, excels at writing about alternative futures. Her latest sci-fi book, Exit Party, is no exception. According to Laura Miller, Mandel expertly weaves together an interconnected cast of characters in this “lovely, meditative new novel.” Tune in to a nuanced conversation about sex work: On the latest episode of Death, Sex & Money, host Anna Sale spoke with Anna Blake, an OnlyFans creator who had hoped the platform would be a path to financial stability. Instead, the experience forced her to change her relationship to both sex and money. End the day with some wordplay: What’s an 11-letter word for “Hogs”? And what’s a three-letter word for a “Hog’s neighbor, maybe”? Play today’s crossword for some clever clues and interesting trivia tidbits. That’s all from me. Enjoy your night and see you tomorrow! Are We Heading For “Pearl Harbor in Space”?
2026-09-17

Are We Heading For “Pearl Harbor in Space”?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Secretary of the Air Force Troy Meink declared this week that the United States has weapons orbiting in outer space. It was the first time a U.S. official has publicly acknowledged this fact. The questions, which went unaddressed, are what sorts of weapons they are, why he’s announcing this now, and—most important—what it all means for the broader issues of war and peace. Chinese and Russian spokespeople denounced Meink’s speech as a step toward “war in outer space.” This was not only predictable but also a sick joke. Both of those countries have been launching, orbiting, and testing weapons in space—devices designed to disable or destroy U.S. satellites—for more than a decade, to a degree and with a tactical sophistication that far outpaces anything we have done. And they have been doing this without publicly declaring anything. Retired Lt. Gen. John Shaw, who was deputy commander of U.S. Space Command until 2023 and is still active on boards and companies dealing with space security, told me in a phone conversation Wednesday that Meink’s remarks marked “not an escalation but a measured response” to what China and Russia have long been doing. That can’t be said of many of America’s arms races with other nations over the past 80 years, but it seems to be the case with this one. And an arms race in space, of some sort, now appears almost inevitable. For many years, and to an extent far greater than any other country, the U.S. military has depended on assets in space. Photoreconnaissance satellites spy on what adversaries are doing. Early-warning satellites detect attacks. GPS satellites transmit signals that guide “smart bombs” to their targets, connect communications networks, even tell troops on the ground and ships at sea exactly where they are. If some of these satellites get hacked, damaged, derailed, or destroyed, America’s ability to fight a war would be seriously degraded. That seems to be the intention of Russian and Chinese “space control” programs. It is widely thought that if the U.S. ever goes to war with Russia or China, the first shots would be anti-satellite attacks, complemented by cyberattacks against a wide range of computer and communications targets. In a cyberwar, the United States has much bigger stones but much glassier houses. In other words, we have a larger, more destructive array of cyberattack tools. However, our military forces, as well as our economy and social structures, are far more dependent on software networks—and therefore much more vulnerable to cyberattacks. In outer space, according to a former defense and intelligence official who still has high-level access to information on the subject, we are not only more vulnerable but outgunned. “The U.S. is absolutely vulnerable to a Pearl Harbor in space,” this person told me. “We’re getting better, but we’re not remotely able to do what we need to do to respond to the threat.” This former official said he doesn’t understand why Meink gave this speech; he doesn’t see what we gain from it and fears that Moscow or Beijing might respond by boosting their program. However, two retired officers, each of whom has dealt with space security, disagree. First, they doubt that Moscow and Beijing can do more than they’re already doing. Second, they see Meink’s remarks as, in the words of one former officer, “strategic communications—the beginning of a discussion about deterrence.” If that is the case, even the retired officers allow that Meink could have supplied more detail and context. He made his announcement during a Monday speech in National Harbor, Maryland, at the Air, Space, and Cyber Conference of the Air and Space Forces Association—an event that would certainly be covered by specialist publications. Here were his words: We are increasing readiness against existing threats, and the United States has on-orbit space control weapons capable of defending the Joint Force against hostile adversary action. To the general reader, the sentence might seem obscure and may have been so intended. To the aficionado, however, it marked a milestone. Some time ago, U.S. officials publicly said there were systems on the ground that could jam communications to and from satellites. Now Meink was saying we have systems that can do this “on orbit”—that is, while orbiting in outer space. Nor did he mention jammers specifically. He said we had “space-control weapons” (without defining what kind) that could defend “the Joint Force” (meaning the entire array of U.S. armed forces, in space and on Earth). Space Force, the service of the armed forces that deals with this topic, has defined space control as efforts to “destroy, disrupt, or degrade” vital assets in outer space. What kinds of weapons do this? Meink didn’t say, but the range can be inferred from published accounts about the kinds of space-control weapons that Russia or China has launched and tested. These include not just jammers that block or distort a satellite’s uplinks or downlinks, but also “laser dazzlers,” which blind imaging satellites; “co-orbital interceptors,” which collide with, grapple and move, or blow up near targets; and sprays that damage satellite cameras, sensors, or solar panels. It is likely that when Meink stated that we have “on-orbit space-control weapons,” he was referring to those sorts of weapons. He was not referring to President Donald Trump’s Golden Dome missile-defense program—though some elements of it might also apply to space control. Certainly he wasn’t referring to nuclear weapons in outer space, which are banned by the 1967 Outer Space Treaty—and which would be ridiculous anyway, because the blast, radiation, X-rays, and other effects would destroy many of our own satellites, commercial and military. (For the same reason, it’s hard to take seriously reports of Russian test nukes in outer space.) According to GlobalSecurity, a private nonpartisan research organization on defense issues, it’s unlikely Meink was talking about any sorts of “kinetic” weapons—i.e., weapons that unleash bullets or bombs—since the resulting debris could also be self-destructive. (The treaty outlaws “weapons” in space but doesn’t define the term, giving us and other countries a lot of leeway.) In a roundtable after his speech, Meink declined to get more specific except to say that he wouldn’t exclude “kinetic” weapons and that the weapons, whatever they are, could be used for offensive and defensive purposes (a subtle distinction, in any case). Nor would he state how long we’ve had these things in space, how thoroughly they’ve been tested, or other details. Others noted that the main point of publicizing the program is deterrence—to make clear to adversaries that we can respond in kind to any threats they might pose, which can be done without going into specifics on exactly what we can do. Meanwhile, the former defense and intelligence official told me, “Russia and China have been doing all of this. We’ve been doing some of it for decades, but mostly as R&D. Russia and China have been launching these things and testing them in very provocative ways.” He added, “They’ve not only closed the gap—they’ve demonstrated an ability to maneuver satellites and conduct operations that we cannot do.” Not only that; military agencies aren’t able even to detect some of this activity. “Commercial satellite companies, which provide the government with services, have detected Chinese activities and have alerted us. Otherwise, we’d be clueless about a lot of what’s going on.” This evolving and now possibly accelerating arms race in space should come as no surprise. Once a realm of life becomes a “domain” for military activity, it’s all but inevitable that weapons will be deployed to protect it. Over the decades, this has happened in the air, in the oceans, and in cyberspace, so why not in space? Back in 2011, President Barack Obama signed the National Security Space Strategy, declaring that outer space—previously considered a peaceful sanctuary—was “congested, contested, and competitive.” (Actually, this too merely acknowledged what had been true for some time.) It was hoped that conflicts could be managed through diplomacy or, if that didn’t work out, through military strategy. In 2014 and 2020, U.S. and Russian diplomats met to discuss possible treaties. Shaw, then still on active duty, led the American delegation. He later wrote an op-ed for the Wall Street Journal lamenting that “the Russia delegation was merely groping for leverage against the U.S., its allies, and China.” Talks were going to continue, but Vladimir Putin’s invasion of Ukraine in 2022 put an end to that chance. China has since pulled ahead as the dominant player in this new arms race. Will Trump and Xi Jinping discuss the issue at their Washington summit next week? Doubtful, given the more urgent issues on the table—and, likely, both leaders’ reluctance to place limits on any competitive activity. As is the case with A.I., it is a shame that just as a tempting technology is advancing to new levels at once alluring and dangerous, world politics are spinning out of control, relations between the major powers are deteriorating, and the United States—at one time the country best placed to moderate at least some of these tensions—is led by a president who not only is clueless on these matters but thinks he’s a genius. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump Is Aiming Again at a Familiar First-Term Target. The Consequences Could Be Deadly.
2026-09-17

Trump Is Aiming Again at a Familiar First-Term Target. The Consequences Could Be Deadly.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Last week, the Trump administration proposed a regulation that, if not undone by the president taking office in 2029, would end the uninterrupted practice—one almost as old as our nation—of collecting race and ethnicity in the decennial census. This proposal would impair public health and cost numerous Americans their lives. The most widely reported part of the regulation was the decision not to count noncitizens without green cards, a change that could have radical implications for future elections and billions in federal funding. But the race and ethnicity change is also important and could trigger large implications for public health. As we have argued in recent academic research, race and ethnicity data are key in understanding population health outcomes. Our article shows that racial health disparities come from both differences in exposure to environmental stressors and different underlying vulnerability stemming from, for instance, genetics or access to healthcare. The resulting differences can be stark. For instance, Black children are 60 percent more likely than U.S. children overall to have asthma and four and a half times more likely to die from it. Hispanic women have a 40 percent higher incidence of cervical cancer and 20 percent higher death rate from it compared to non-Hispanic white women. And Asian Americans have extremely elevated rates of certain diseases, including tuberculosis and hepatitis B. For many diseases, it is impossible or impracticable to collect detailed, local-level prevalence data. Government agencies have responded by using race and ethnicity as a proxy or input into models determining the prevalence of these health conditions. Most notably, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention combine survey data with census race and ethnicity estimates to model the prevalence of a number of chronic diseases—including asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, and cancer—at a fine-grained geographic level. That granular data allows decisionmakers to allocate resources and target outreach to the areas with the highest need. For instance, Michigan has established a pilot program to place mobile health unit vans in at-risk areas. Private health systems also use this type of data frequently, with one medical system using it to concentrate services in four high-risk neighborhoods of New York City. Without collecting race and ethnicity data in the census, these estimates could not be produced, needlessly wasting resources and endangering lives. And the Trump-proposed rule makes this pernicious move despite centuries of contrary historical practice. The practice of collecting race and ethnicity data is as old as the census itself. The 1790 census differentiated between “Free White” individuals and all others, and race has appeared in every census since. The White House Office of Management and Budget promulgated the first government-wide standards in 1977, which have been used by every administration during the past five decades. The Biden administration made long-needed changes to the standards in 2024, including adding a “Middle Eastern or North African” category. That revision was undertaken with analytical rigor: The process took two years, involved 35 agencies, incorporated extensive new and existing research, and elicited over 20,000 comments. Meanwhile, the census’s proposed rule has no scientific justification or participation by federal agencies with experience in this area. And the little rationale the rule does provide does not withstand scrutiny. The Trump administration first argued that lowering the number of questions would increase response rates. Unlike the meticulous, empirically based Biden-era revision, the Trump rule cites only two studies for this proposition: One is over 30 years old, and the other is not about the census. Not to mention that the Trump proposal would simply swap out the race question for one on citizenship—making clear that the concern over response rates is a subterfuge. Second, the proposed rule suggests the collection of racial data may be unconstitutional under recent Supreme Court decisions in Students for Fair Admissions v. President and Fellows of Harvard College and Louisiana v. Callais. While both decisions cover the topic of race-based preference, neither even remotely addresses the constitutional status of data collections. Callais concerned the role of race in drawing legislative districts under the Voting Rights Act, while SFFA involved racial preferences in college admissions. Courts have made clear that collection of racial data does not raise the same constitutional concerns as racially based preferences. Even a colorblind administration should still be invested in avoiding preventable deaths. Finally, the rule argues that the collection of race and ethnicity data would continue through the Census Bureau’s yearly American Community Survey. But the two are complements, not substitutes. The ACS has an annual sample size of only 3.5 million, whereas the decennial census has universal reach. That smaller sample means certain demographic groups may be over- or under-covered, biasing the total estimates. To mitigate this problem, researchers adjust the data using the demographic data derived from the decennial census, a process that would be impossible without the race and ethnicity question. By heedlessly seeking to end a centurieslong practice, the Trump administration is headed down a dangerous path. It will compromise our ability to provide effective healthcare, lead to underinvestment in the communities that need it the most, and cost American lives. Admins, you've been warned: The clock is ticking on Edge's IE Mode
2026-09-18

Admins, you've been warned: The clock is ticking on Edge's IE Mode

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Legacy web apps have guaranteed shelter only through 2029, and Redmond warns modernization can take years Microsoft has reminded administrators that Internet Explorer mode in Edge is guaranteed support only through 2029, so it is time to get those legacy migrations moving. Internet Explorer mode lets enterprises continue using legacy websites within Edge rather than relying on the retired Internet Explorer browser. It uses the Trident MSHTML engine from Internet Explorer 11 and permits elements such as ActiveX controls, although other Internet Explorer functionality, such as custom toolbars, is not supported. Microsoft has promised to support IE mode until at least the end of 2029 and to provide one year's notice before retiring it. The feature is unlikely to stop working immediately after support ends, but organizations that depend on it could face security, compatibility, and compliance risks. A message in the Microsoft 365 admin center urges customers to begin modernizing legacy systems that still depend on Internet Explorer technology. The company wrote: "Application modernization projects can take years to complete depending on the number and complexity of dependencies, so early planning reduces the risk of disruption later." Microsoft has been banging this particular drum for some time. It announced in 2021 that IE mode would be supported until at least the end of 2029, but its latest reminder suggests some customers still have substantial migration work ahead of them. Administrators do not need to act immediately, but migrations involving old and heavily dependent applications can take years to complete. Internet Explorer debuted with the Plus! Pack for Windows 95 in 1995, but traces of it will survive for years yet. Internet Explorer 11 is included with Windows 10 IoT Enterprise LTSC 2021, which remains supported until 2032, although Microsoft could retire Edge's IE mode considerably sooner. KDE turns 30 and someone's brought an AI-native desktop proposal
2026-09-18

KDE turns 30 and someone's brought an AI-native desktop proposal

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Akademy talk imagines Plasma assembling itself around a personal model of each user KDE's annual conference takes place this weekend at Graz University of Technology in Austria, with an AI-native desktop proposal likely to divide attendees. The KDE project is celebrating its 30th anniversary this year, giving delegates at the Akademy conference in Graz another reason to raise a glass. The conference begins on Saturday, September 19, and registration remains open. Although Xfce 1.0 appeared slightly earlier, its initial releases were proprietary, making what was originally known as the Kool Desktop Environment one of the earliest entirely FOSS desktop environments for Linux. Version 1.0 was released in July 1998. Although created for Linux, KDE software now runs on several other operating systems. The potentially contentious proposal comes in a Sunday afternoon talk entitled What would it take? A lovable, sovereign, AI-native KDE, presented by longtime KDE contributors Eva Brucherseifer and Jan Muehlig. In 2003, they conducted a usability study of the then newly released KDE 3.1. Of the 60 office workers tested, none of whom had prior Linux experience, 87 percent said they enjoyed working with KDE. The talk has three parts: what KDE learned – or failed to learn – from that study; the case for an "AI-native" desktop; and the importance of a sovereign European computing stack. It is the middle section that may ruffle feathers. The outline says: "A desktop that loves you back has to know you. Personal AI has crossed the threshold where the desktop itself can be compiled per user, per device, per moment, from a small portable model of the user – what we are sketching as Kadai: an encrypted, vendor-agnostic personal kernel from which Plasma assembles each Activity. "Plasma is uniquely well-fit for this – Activities, Plasmoids, the existing scripting surface – but a handful of upstream changes (declarative reconciliation, per-widget capabilities, richer Activity metadata) would turn KDE into the first shell that treats AI as infrastructure rather than as a chat box bolted to the side." This is very much not what this vulture wants from a desktop computer. It may win admirers, but seems more likely to polarize the audience. Writer and AI critic David Gerard points out the proposal's resemblance to the Resonant Computing Manifesto, launched in December 2025 at Wired's Big Interview in San Francisco. Gerard criticized the manifesto later that month. Gerard also notes that contributors to the Trinity Desktop Environment, a fork of KDE 3, have begun dabbling with AI-generated code – potentially disappointing those who viewed Trinity as an escape route. Microsoft fixes the fix that broke Excel paste – partly

Hotfix covers only Excel 2016 and may still come unstuck around conditional formatting Microsoft has issued a partial fix for the Excel bug that causes paste operations to fail. The hotfix applies only to Excel 2016, and Microsoft warns that pasting may still fail in workbooks containing conditional formatting. Earlier in September, Microsoft released a security update for Excel to address remote code execution and information disclosure vulnerabilities. It later acknowledged that the update had introduced another problem: "In Microsoft Excel 2024, 2021, 2019, and 2016, the paste operation might fail silently. "Although users try to paste content, the source remains selected and the destination is unmodified. The September Windows security update required an out-of-band release to address several problems, but even that left some work unfinished. While users of newer Excel releases continue to wait, Microsoft has at least repaired another problem introduced by an August update. A bug that caused Windows Security to incorrectly report that Microsoft Defender Antivirus was disabled was resolved in a Defender update released on September 17. The ideal database for AI agents doesn't exist yet, says Percona CEO
2026-09-18

The ideal database for AI agents doesn't exist yet, says Percona CEO

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Iterative workloads caught vendors off guard, but requirements for a clean-slate solution remain unclear INTERVIEW Percona CEO Peter Farkas reckons AI caught the database industry off guard and that agentic workloads may eventually require a new kind of database – once anyone works out what those workloads actually need. Farkas spoke to The Register at the company's Percona Live event in Amsterdam, where members of the often-fractious open source database community gathered to discuss best practices, case studies, and the future. Percona provides software and support across a growing range of open source database technologies. Over its 20-year history, it has worked with MySQL, MongoDB, PostgreSQL, Valkey, and Redis. MySQL's first release arrived in 1995. The PostgreSQL project marked its 30th anniversary in July 2026, although its roots in the POSTGRES project stretch back further. Both databases therefore have decades behind them and represent mature, stable options – just as the conservative world of database administration prefers. The surge in AI services, however, is introducing workloads that these systems were not designed around, and Farkas believes none of the current choices are ideal. Of the current selection, Farkas said: "It's definitely not the best that we can have. It's the thing that we have right now." "I think AI definitely caught the industry off guard," he said. Farkas pointed to "iteration-focused workloads" in which an agent might make 150 attempts before a person or another system selects the best result. "A human would probably not push 150 options just so someone or another AI can pick out the winning one," he said. For now, organizations must select technology capable of handling these workloads without assuming that today's choice will remain the best fit. Farkas was careful not to favor a particular database. "Everything needs a database," Farkas said, though coming from the CEO of Percona, that is perhaps unsurprising. "If the new buzzword is blockchain or NFT or cloud or whatever, behind all of those things, there's a database, and AI is no exception. "AI needs a database, and AI is going to use the databases that we have for the time being, but I do believe that the best solution for AI or agentic workloads does not exist at this point, as there's just not enough time for something to become as mature as the trusted database software." The rapid pace of change sits uneasily with a database industry accustomed to products maturing over decades. AI development, by contrast, still has the feel of technology being flung at a wall to see what sticks. Farkas laughed when asked when such a solution might emerge. "I don't have a crystal ball," he said. He believes the AI market must mature before its requirements become clear enough for anyone to build a clean-slate database designed around them. Organizations are still deciding where AI belongs in their technology stacks, he said, including whether to run models themselves or use cloud services, and whether every use case needs a large language model rather than a smaller model with a lighter footprint. "It's easy to experiment with something that can just do everything, but that also means that the resources being consumed are outsized compared to the result," Farkas said. Farkas became CEO of Percona in October 2025, succeeding Ann Schlemmer. He had previously worked at Percona as director of support until 2016. In December 2021, he co-founded FerretDB, an open source service that converts MongoDB wire protocol queries into SQL for PostgreSQL or SQLite. MongoDB sued FerretDB in May 2025, alleging patent infringement, false advertising, and trademark misuse. FerretDB disputes the allegations. Asked what lessons he had brought from FerretDB, Farkas replied: "Lots of PTSD from courtrooms." Farkas also brings experience of working across multiple open source communities – useful as Percona shifts from a company primarily associated with MySQL to a database-agnostic, multi-tech business. Doing so has its own challenges. He noted that different communities work in markedly different ways. "We had multiple attempts at adding Postgres to our portfolio," he recalled, "but we had some wrong ideas that were based on the MySQL experience on how to engage with that community." "They have very different preferences. They have very different red flags they are looking for. For example, if you call something 'Percona TDE for Postgres', that is not going to fly: 'What is this nonsense? This is vendor lock-in! This is just one company trying to push their crap!' Whereas in the MySQL space, that's totally normal, and we needed to unlearn these things." Could Percona eventually extend its open source approach to AI? "Frankly, AI is a vendor lock-in situation," Farkas replied, pointing to services such as Gemini and ChatGPT. He argued that there is not yet a mature, enterprise-ready way to run open-weight agents, although Percona is exploring what it might contribute in that area. "Percona is not anti-AI," he said. "Percona is anti-rebranding Postgres or MongoDB as the AI database." ® Legacy web apps have guaranteed shelter only through 2029, and Redmond warns modernization can take years Akademy talk imagines Plasma assembling itself around a personal model of each user PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Hotfix covers only Excel 2016 and may still come unstuck around conditional formatting Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Former Labour deputy Tom Watson joins Palantir as £330M NHS deal nears break point
2026-09-18

Former Labour deputy Tom Watson joins Palantir as £330M NHS deal nears break point

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
US spy-tech firm insists its new UK senior vice president is there to 'build things, not to open doors' Former Labour deputy leader Tom Watson has taken a senior UK role at Palantir as the government considers whether to invoke a break clause in the US spy-tech company's controversial £330 million National Health Service (NHS) contract. The appointment is the latest high-profile example of Palantir recruiting from British politics and public service as it seeks to expand its government business. Palantir chief executive Alex Karp said in a statement: "This is just the latest chapter of Tom's 40-year fight for better public services. We are fortunate to have him guide us as we help the British government to deliver for the British public." Watson, who has advised Palantir since 2024, will become its UK senior vice president and work alongside Louis Mosley, executive vice president for the UK and Europe. In a statement shared on social media, Mosley said: "Tom will lead our work on what Palantir owes this country. That means answering the Prime Minister's challenge to companies holding public contracts: support British jobs, invest in skills, deliver in every postcode. It also means helping people prepare for what AI does to work and to the services they rely on." Mosley said Watson had been on leave from the House of Lords since March and would remain so while working for Palantir. He has also surrendered his parliamentary pass and the access that accompanies it. "Tom has joined Palantir to build things, not to open doors," he added. The assurances anticipate concerns about the revolving door between government and technology companies, particularly as Palantir's major public sector contracts face political scrutiny. In April, Zubir Ahmed MP, a junior minister at the Department of Health and Social Care, said the government could end Palantir's seven-year Federated Data Platform (FDP) contract when the break clause becomes available next spring. "My north star is always patient safety and quality, and of course value for money. If, at the point of the break clause, we evaluate and find that there are other providers that can do the job better, then of course that needs to be looked at and reflected upon," he said. However, replacing the platform provider at relatively short notice could prove difficult. The House of Commons Health and Social Care Committee said in July that the Department of Health and Social Care should begin preparing to replace the FDP before the contract reaches its break clause in February. Palantir began working with the NHS during the pandemic and received £60 million in contracts without competition, including work that later fed into the FDP. KPMG tech cuts come with a severance sum some staff call insulting
2026-09-18

KPMG tech cuts come with a severance sum some staff call insulting

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
AI, Cyber, SAP and Testing teams caught in latest reshaping of Big Four consultancy's Advisory arm KPMG UK is laying off staff from the Tech and Data areas of its Advisory division and offering severance terms that one affected employee described as "insulting and disgraceful." The job cuts, affecting about 4 percent of the Advisory staff, were announced internally in July. Affected workers in the AI, Cyber, SAP, and Testing teams are scheduled to leave the consulting business next month. Insiders have now shared with The Register details of the payments offered to those set to depart. The proposed redundancy package, subject to consultation and the employee signing a settlement agreement, combines statutory redundancy pay with several enhancements. The document helpfully advises staff to divide their annual salary by 52 to calculate their weekly pay. The number of weeks to which each employee is entitled depends on their age during each full year of service: half a week's pay for each year worked while under 22, one week's pay for each year worked from age 22 to 40, and 1.5 weeks' pay for each year worked from age 41. "If you turned 41 while working at KPMG, the higher rate of 1.5 weeks' pay only applies to the full year of service completed after you turned 41," KPMG's missive states. "Redundancy pay is calculated based on the individual's number of years' service (up to a maximum of 20 years' service). If you've been at KPMG for more than 20 years, your most recent 20 years of service are considered when determining the number of weeks' pay you will be entitled to." KPMG's enhancements remove the statutory weekly pay cap of £751 and count partial years of service exceeding six months. Regardless of their length of service, staff are guaranteed either £1,250 plus statutory redundancy pay or eight weeks' pay inclusive of statutory redundancy pay, whichever is higher. The agreement also provides pay in lieu of notice, including employer pension contributions and, where applicable, a car allowance, according to the document. The document explaining the package includes an illustration of three employees alongside examples of the terms they could receive. Two of the illustrated employees appear delighted. The reaction among actual employees was less cheerful. One affected worker suspected that "protecting the profit pot for the equity partners is what is driving this re-org mostly." KPMG's UK partners received an average of £880,000 in the year to September 2025. "The payout scale is insulting and disgraceful," one affected employee told The Register. A KPMG spokesperson said: "As our market evolves, we are adapting where we are focusing and how we are set up to make sure we have the right skills in place to best serve our clients. To respond to these market dynamics combined with low levels of attrition, we are proposing reductions in some of our advisory client-facing teams and will support our colleagues throughout this process." The latest cuts come six months after KPMG eliminated 600 roles across its UK organization, including around 120 in Advisory. "The firm's consulting business has been under pressure for some time as market demand has remained subdued," said Duncan Aitchison, an analyst at TechMarketView, who estimated that the practice's revenue fell 9 percent between fiscal 2024 and 2025. "KPMG is not the only Big Four firm taking action to reshape its consulting business. In April 2026, PwC UK unveiled plans to bring together two of its three advisory businesses as part of a global effort to respond to the disruptions to the consulting industry caused by AI." ® Legacy web apps have guaranteed shelter only through 2029, and Redmond warns modernization can take years Akademy talk imagines Plasma assembling itself around a personal model of each user PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Hotfix covers only Excel 2016 and may still come unstuck around conditional formatting Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Security researchers used Claude to help them hack into OpenAI
2026-09-18

Security researchers used Claude to help them hack into OpenAI

The Verge | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
A three-person team of researchers used a corrupted image file and forum software to hack into OpenAI. A three-person team of researchers used a corrupted image file and forum software to hack into OpenAI. A team of three independent security researchers at Hacktron says it took less than 72 hours for them to hack into OpenAI employee accounts using Anthropic’s Claude Opus 4.8 and 5, the Wall Street Journal reports. They were able to access OpenAI’s GitHub repository, called “Monorepo,” which reportedly contains “OpenAI’s algorithmic secrets,” according to the Wall Street Journal’s sources. They stopped short of accessing internal code in Monorepo themselves, but sent a pull request from an employee’s Codex account to prove they gained access. They were able to get in through Discourse, the third-party service that hosts OpenAI’s community forums, by exploiting an issue with the system it uses to process HEIF images. According to Hacktron, Claude Opus 5 launched in the evening on July 24th, and by 10AM the next day they had used it to achieve RCE on Discourse Cloud and accessed OpenAI’s instance. Their HEIF Heist project took “only one or two days” to adapt to different companies, including OpenAI, Slack, Meta, GitHub Ent, Rails, Next.js, ImageMagick and others, using less than $3,000 in tokens, and to their knowledge, was only detected by one target, Shopify. The vulnerabilities Hacktron reported to Discourse and OpenAI have since been fixed, and Hacktron says OpenAI paid it $6,500 for finding the bug, but as Hacktron CTO Mohan Pedhapati said to the WSJ, “I don’t think we are as strong as Chinese threat actors… We’re just three guys with Claude and Codex subscriptions.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. This cartridge-playing Game Boy clone is smaller and cheaper than Analogue’s Pocket

The FPGB Mini is only slightly wider than the original Nintendo cartridges it plays. The FPGB Mini is only slightly wider than the original Nintendo cartridges it plays. FunnyPlaying, a Chinese company known for its Game Boy and GBA upgrade kits, has announced a new handheld that plays original Nintendo cartridges and ROMs. The FPGB Mini’s cartridge support is limited to the Game Boy and Game Boy Color so it isn’t as capable as the Analogue Pocket that’s also compatible with the GBA and Sega Game Gear. What still makes the FPGB Mini a tempting alternative is its extremely pocketable design resulting in a handheld only slightly wider than a Game Boy cartridge, and its cheaper price. Following recent price hikes the Analogue Pocket is now $239.99 while the FPGB Mini is currently discounted from $89.99 to $69.99 for the first batch of just 20 units that quickly sold out. However, the company is potentially planning to release more following some hardware improvements, according to Retro Dodo who’s been testing the handheld for a few days and so far seems impressed with its design and capabilities. Like the Pocket the FPGB Mini uses field-programmable gate array (FPGA) technology to play Game Boy and GBC games as accurately as Nintendo’s original hardware does. But the FPGB Mini brings improvements like a 2.98-inch, 800x720-pixel LCD display, an 1,800mAh rechargeable battery, and a microSD slot for storing game saves, screenshots, or ROMs that can be played through emulation. The handheld’s compact size does prevent cartridges with rumble motors or other hardware additions from being used, but otherwise the FPGB Mini looks to be about as small as the Game Boy is ever going to get. The Verge
Flash floods can strike without warning — this new technology could change that
2026-09-18

Flash floods can strike without warning — this new technology could change that

The Verge | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
﻿Satellite data combined with machine learning gives meteorologists new tools to predict deadly floods sooner. ﻿Satellite data combined with machine learning gives meteorologists new tools to predict deadly floods sooner. On the morning of June 9th, Laura Lin was working from her home in Lanesville, a rural southern Indiana town about 15 miles from the Kentucky border. She was on a Zoom call, unaware that the heavy rain outside was beginning to flood her yard. “I look over to where the barn is over there, and I see pieces of my wood floating, and I was like, ‘What?’ And I immediately was like, ‘I have to go.’ Close my laptop, and I get my kids up, and I’m like, ‘Something’s wrong,’” Lin recalls. Lin and her family got out safely and sheltered at a neighbor’s house, but Lanesville got over 8 inches of rain within just a few hours that day, way over the threshold for what is considered “heavy” rainfall. The water was gone a few hours later. Floods are the second-deadliest type of weather event in the United States and the deadliest globally. Just 6 inches of fast-flowing water can knock an adult off their feet, 12 inches can lift a car, and 2 feet can move larger vehicles like trucks and SUVs. A warming climate means more instances of extreme rainfall in the US, creating more opportunities for floods to occur. The deadly flash flooding in Nepal in August caused by a glacier collapse shows the power of water to devastate communities worldwide. Fortunately, new tools are being developed to counter this threat. A new software called the Transient Artifact and Continuous Learning System (TACLS) uses satellites and machine learning to try to spot areas that could flood sooner and help the National Weather Service (NWS) make better decisions when it comes to issuing flash flood alerts. “We didn’t get the ‘get on your roof’ warnings until I was already at that person’s house,” Lin says. “All the people in town were already flooded when they started sending out alerts, being like, ‘Get on your roof. We’re dispatching boats.’ So it was way too late.” TACLS has the potential to help change that. “It will help you save lives,” says Ivory Small, science and operations officer at the NWS San Diego Weather Forecast Office. Without TACLS, Small says, a storm moving into your area “could kill some folks.” With TACLS, “you can put out the warning and save some folks.” Weather forecast offices are responsible for issuing severe weather alerts, including flash flood warnings. There are 122 of them throughout the US and its territories, says Jayme Laber, senior service hydrologist for the NWS Weather Forecast Office in Oxnard, California, which oversees the LA area. Small and Laber worked closely on the TACLS project with a broader team of scientists and researchers, and they hope this new project will streamline weather alerts. To understand TACLS, it’s important to first understand how the NWS issues flood alerts. Currently, the people working in weather forecast offices use a variety of tools to monitor flood conditions. They rely on rain and stream gauges to tell them how much water is in a certain place at any given time, Laber says. They track developing storms using satellites and weather radars. The staff monitors rainfall, but their computers also alert them when it rains more than what is considered safe for a specific area. “Based on the type of soils we have, the steepness of slopes, we have developed flash flood guidance so that we know that if we get this amount of rain in this amount of time in that area, it could result in flash flooding,” Laber says. All of that, plus the years of expertise inside those buildings, determines whether or not someone presses a button to issue a flood alert for their area, Laber says. A watch is the least serious alert. It means that there’s a chance a flash flood could happen. The watch might be issued anywhere from 12 to 48 hours in advance, Laber explains. He says local emergency teams should start working on “flood protection measures” when the flood watch is issued, so there’s enough time to prepare before people are stuck and forced to find higher ground. Response teams might review evacuation plans, set up sandbags and road barriers, and identify the highest risk areas ahead of a potential flood. An advisory is the next level up. It might be flooding, “but it’s more nuisance-level. It’s not going to threaten life or property,” Laber says. A warning is the most serious alert. “That’s a life-and-death possibility of being swept off your feet [or] being run over by a car floating down the stream,” Small says. The NWS differentiates a regular flood from a flash flood based on time, says Small. If an area floods in under six hours, it’s a flash flood. The NWS has a lot of different ways to determine whether or not it needs to issue a flash flood warning, but its tech has limitations, says Yehuda Bock, TACLS project lead and research geodesist at the Scripps Institution of Oceanography at the University of California, San Diego. The satellites the NWS uses are more detailed over oceans than land, he says. And tracking rainfall as it’s happening doesn’t give you enough lead time, “because once there’s precipitation, you’re already in the event itself,” he explains. Deserts and other less populated areas typically have fewer rain gauges, Laber says. “We have a fairly dense network [of rain gauges], but it doesn’t cover every inch of the Earth.” TACLS uses satellites and machine learning to identify places on a map — for now, just in California — that risk transitioning from rain to dangerous flash floods. But soon, the technology will be available to every NWS office. Scientists from UCSD, the NWS, and NASA worked together to create TACLS. NASA’s Earth Science Technology Office funded the project through its Advanced Information Systems Technology program. TACLS relies on the Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS), which is primarily used to predict earthquakes, says Bock. But the GNSS network, which consists of satellites and ground sensors, also happens to provide data that can help forecasters decide if they need to issue a flash flood warning. The more water vapor, or moisture, there is in the atmosphere, the longer the delay in communication between the GNSS satellites and the sensors on the ground. The amount of moisture, or “precipitable water,” in the atmosphere tells the NWS what is actually happening in the skies leading up to a storm, Laber says. TACLS uses satellites and machine learning to identify places that risk transitioning from rain to dangerous flash floods. Before TACLS, Laber and his colleagues had to rely on the forecast, which wasn’t always accurate. TACLS provides real-time data about the precipitable water in the atmosphere to compare to the forecast. This helps them determine whether the storm is actually moving at the speed predicted by the weather models. “It helps us to make better decisions in our warning decision process,” Laber says. Even with the GNSS moisture data, forecasters still need something to analyze all of it. Bhavik Chandna, a UCSD graduate student, spent about a year developing the machine learning side of TACLS using what’s called long short-term memory architecture. According to Chandna, this specific machine learning system works especially well for things like developing storms and other weather patterns that change over time. Years of atmospheric measurements from the GNSS network, as well as data about atmospheric rivers, precipitation, and flash flood warnings, were used to train the machine learning model to understand moisture changes in the atmosphere “as a storm develops,” Chandna explains. At the same time, the machine learning system was trained to determine if the conditions are right to issue a flash flood warning. “Machine learning sucks at some things and excels at some things,” says Joel Johnson, associate professor in the department of earth and planetary sciences at the University of Texas at Austin. Johnson, who was not involved in the TACLS project, knows firsthand the challenges of working with these types of tools. “In general, it’s very easy to have artifacts in your data, false positives. A sensor goes funky, and things like that,” Johnson says. Still, he says TACLS seems “well suited” to the task of predicting flash floods. False positives do happen, Chandna says. “A real weather system usually affects an area, not just one isolated GNSS station. If one station shows a strong signal but its neighbors do not, that detection can be suppressed. If several nearby stations see the same pattern, we have much more confidence that it represents a real atmospheric signal,” he adds. Chandna says TACLS is not intended to replace the forecasters who issue weather alerts. It’s just another piece of information that can assist in their decision-making, he says. TACLS software is already being used in the LA and San Diego weather forecast offices where flash flooding is a common occurrence, says Laber. “In the West, we deal with a lot more flash flooding, where it happens a lot quicker,” he explains. Today, TACLS shows them a graph with various points telling them what is going on in real time. A newer version of TACLS with improved graphics is currently being loaded onto the NWS system and is nearly done, Laber says. It will be available to all of the NWS offices in the second half of October, he adds. This iteration has a map with detailed graphics showing how much it’s raining and whether an extreme weather event is occurring. According to Laber, having more tools — like this latest update to TACLS — will further improve their decision-making when it comes to issuing flash flood alerts, and, in turn, maybe speed up alert times so people know there’s a problem sooner. Laber says TACLS will likely be used the most in the Western US because the majority of the GNSS sensors are located in places known for earthquake activity. But Chandna thinks TACLS could work well elsewhere too. As long as there are some GNSS sensors and available local weather data, “we could use the same ideology that we have in our system around different parts of the world,” he says. Small is thinking beyond TACLS. He believes ongoing research is crucial to improving how the NWS issues flash flood warnings. “We learn what’s going on a little more every time we do a storm because they’re all different,” he says. “It’s imperative that you do the research after, especially big events, and see what caused it to do what it did, and why, if it was different than what we already knew, did this occur.” Lin’s concern is Lanesville and her home. “It would have been helpful to know [water was coming] before I was locked in. I couldn’t go anywhere,” Lin says. “If I got an alert that was like, ‘You need to get out now if you’re in lower-lying ground, this is coming.’ Yeah, that would have [been helpful]. But it came in so fast.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

Waymo says it will launch a robotaxi service in Singapore in 2028, as the Alphabet-owned company continues to eye overseas markets for expansion. Waymo’s vehicles will begin arriving in Singapore in “the coming months,” the company says, in preparation of mapping and autonomous testing with human safety drivers behind the wheel in 2027. Waymo says it is “working toward offering fully autonomous ride-hailing to the public” by 2028. Waymo will need approval from Singapore’s Land Transport Authority (LTA) before it can begin accepting passengers. The city-state requires all autonomous vehicles to undergo a safety assessment conducted at the Centre of Excellence for Testing and Research (CETRAN) before they can be deployed on public roads. The tests have been developed by LTA and CETRAN, with input from the Traffic Police. But Waymo isn’t totally unknown in Singapore. The company established a corporate entity there earlier this year and participates in Singapore’s Steering Committee on Autonomous Vehicles. Singapore has become a hotbed for autonomous vehicle testing. The local authorities have greenlit autonomous freight delivery and road-sweepers, as well as self-driving buses for airport workers. Motional, a US-based company majority owned by Hyundai, conducted a robotaxi pilot in Singapore in 2016. In addition to Singapore, Waymo has said it plans on launching a robotaxi service in London by the end of 2026, and Tokyo in 2027. The company has also said it will bring its robotaxis to Munich, Germany, in late 2027, marking its first expansion in the European Union. “Singapore has built one of the safest, most efficient, and most forward-thinking transportation ecosystems in the world,” Waymo co-CEO Tekedra Mawakana said in a statement. “Their focus on reliability, sustainability, and human-centric progress directly mirrors how we have built Waymo’s technology and service.” This year, Waymo has announced several new domestic markets, including Nashville, Denver, San Diego, and Las Vegas. The company has around 4,000 vehicles operating in about 15 US cities. But regulatory hurdles and political opposition have kept it out of some of the US’ most important cities, including New York City, Chicago, and Washington, DC. As the robotaxi debate continues to rage on in America, Waymo has signaled that it intends to keep growing overseas — with no signs of slowing down. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. On Wednesday, MIT Technology Review hosted a live Roundtables event that asked the question many seem to be asking right now: could AI really kill us all? But attendees had more questions than we had time to answer, so we asked senior AI editor Will Douglas Heaven and AI reporter Grace Huckins to tackle some of the best ones. The questions they tried to answer include: am I going to die? Why should AI kill us, if at all? Is AI really dangerous, or is it just tech companies drumming up PR? And what steps can be taken to make sure AI is controlled, monitored and regulated effectively? One of the ways AI could potentially cause catastrophic harm is by aiding the design and creation of bioweapons. In 2022, researchers found that it was remarkably easy to do this with an AI “molecule generator” built to develop drugs. In less than six hours, the model generated 40,000 molecules that could serve as chemical warfare agents. Today, AI tools can answer questions on almost every area of science, while advances in gene editing and synthetic biology have made biotech tools more accessible. There are safeguards, but none are ironclad. However, scientists disagree about how serious the risk is anyway. Find out why it’s easier than ever to design killer pathogens.
